**Conclusion.** $(x,y)\in\{(\sqrt{5/2},-\sqrt{5/2}),(-\sqrt{5/2},\sqrt{5/2})\}$.

@@@ equations-cubic-real.md
---
title: A cubic with three roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: cubic, factoring, roots
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$x^3-6x^2+11x-6=0.$$

## Hint

Test small integers.

## Solution

**Test.** $x=1$: $1-6+11-6=0$. Root. $x=2$: $8-24+22-6=0$. Root. $x=3$: $27-54+33-6=0$. Root.

**Factor.** $x^3-6x^2+11x-6=(x-1)(x-2)(x-3)$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{1,2,3\}$.

@@@ equations-radical-parameter-deep.md
---
title: A radical equation with parameter (advanced)
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: parameter, radical equation, discussion
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all real $a$ such that the equation
$$\sqrt{x^2+ax+1}=x-1$$
has a real solution.

## Hint

Square carefully: the RHS must be $\ge0$, and the resulting quadratic must have a root satisfying the condition.

## Solution

**Condition.** $x\ge1$.

**Squaring.** $x^2+ax+1=x^2-2x+1$, i.e. $ax=-2x$, i.e. $(a+2)x=0$.

**Cases.**
- If $a=-2$: the equation is satisfied for all $x$ with $x\ge1$. So any $x\ge1$ works. Also need $x^2+ax+1\ge0$: $x^2-2x+1=(x-1)^2\ge0$, always. So $a=-2$ works.
- If $a\neq-2$: $x=0$, but we need $x\ge1$. Impossible.

**Conclusion.** The only value is $a=-2$.

@@@ equations-system-symmetric-3.md
---
title: A symmetric 3-variable system
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: symmetric system, Vieta, olympiad
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}^3$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x+y+z=6\\ xy+yz+zx=11\\ xyz=6\end{cases}$$

## Hint

$x,y,z$ are the roots of $t^3-6t^2+11t-6=0$.

## Solution

**Polynomial.** By Vieta's formulas, $x,y,z$ are roots of
$$t^3-St^2+Qt-P=0$$
with $S=6$, $Q=11$, $P=6$:
$$t^3-6t^2+11t-6=0.$$

**Factor.** $t=1$ is a root: $1-6+11-6=0$. Divide: $t^3-6t^2+11t-6=(t-1)(t^2-5t+6)=(t-1)(t-2)(t-3)$.

**Roots.** $\{x,y,z\}=\{1,2,3\}$ (in any order).

**Conclusion.** The solutions are the six permutations of $(1,2,3)$.

@@@ equations-exponential-substitution.md
---
title: An exponential equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: exponential equation, substitution, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$4^x-5\cdot2^x+4=0.$$

## Hint

Set $t=2^x>0$.

## Solution

**Substitution.** $t=2^x>0$, so $4^x=t^2$. The equation becomes
$$t^2-5t+4=0,$$
i.e. $(t-1)(t-4)=0$, so $t=1$ or $t=4$.

**Back.** $2^x=1\Rightarrow x=0$; $2^x=4\Rightarrow x=2$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{0,2\}$.

@@@ equations-logarithmic.md
---
title: A logarithmic equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: logarithmic equation, domain, exponential
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\ln(x)+\ln(x-3)=\ln(4).$$

## Hint

Domain: $x>3$. Combine the logarithms.

## Solution

**Domain.** $x>0$ and $x-3>0$, so $x>3$.

**Combine.** $\ln(x(x-3))=\ln4$, i.e. $x(x-3)=4$, i.e. $x^2-3x-4=0$.

**Roots.** $(x-4)(x+1)=0$, so $x=4$ or $x=-1$.

**Check.** $x=-1$ is outside the domain. $x=4$ works.

**Conclusion.** $x=4$.

@@@ equations-trigonometric.md
---
title: A trigonometric equation
category: algebra/equations
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: trigonometric equation, solving, periodicity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $[0,2\pi]$ the equation
$$2\cos^2 x-3\cos x+1=0.$$

## Hint

Set $t=\cos x\in[-1,1]$.

## Solution

**Substitution.** $2t^2-3t+1=0$, i.e. $(2t-1)(t-1)=0$, so $t=1/2$ or $t=1$.

**Case $\cos x=1$:** $x=0$ (in $[0,2\pi]$) or $x=2\pi$.

**Case $\cos x=1/2$:** $x=\pi/3$ or $x=5\pi/3$.

**Conclusion.** $x\in\{0,\pi/3,5\pi/3,2\pi\}$.

@@@ equations-polynomial-divisibility.md
---
title: Polynomial divisibility and roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: polynomial, divisibility, factor theorem
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find the remainder of the division of $P(x)=x^{100}+x^{50}+1$ by $x^2-1$.

## Hint

$P\equiv P\pmod{x^2-1}$ and the remainder has degree $\le1$: write $P=(x^2-1)Q+ax+b$ and evaluate at $x=\pm1$.

## Solution

**Remainder form.** $P=(x^2-1)Q+ax+b$.

**Evaluate at $x=1$.** $P(1)=1+1+1=3=a+b$.

**Evaluate at $x=-1$.** $P(-1)=1+1+1=3=-a+b$.

**Solve.** $a+b=3$, $-a+b=3$, so $a=0$, $b=3$.

**Conclusion.** The remainder is $3$ (constant).

@@@ equations-system-inequality.md
---
title: A system with an inequality
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: system, inequality, integers
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Find all integers $(x,y)$ satisfying
$$\begin{cases}x+y=5\\ x^2+y^2\le13\end{cases}$$

## Hint

List possibilities for $(x,y)$ with $x+y=5$, then check.

## Solution

**From $x+y=5$:** $y=5-x$. Then
$$x^2+(5-x)^2\le13\iff2x^2-10x+25\le13\iff2x^2-10x+12\le0\iff x^2-5x+6\le0.$$
This is $(x-2)(x-3)\le0$, so $x\in[2,3]$. Integer $x\in\{2,3\}$.

**Solutions.** $(2,3)$ and $(3,2)$.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y)\in\{(2,3),(3,2)\}$.

@@@ equations-equation-with-floor.md
---
title: An equation with the floor function
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: floor function, integer part, olympiad
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $\mathbb{R}$ the equation
$$\lfloor x\rfloor + x = 5.$$

## Hint

Write $x=n+r$ with $n=\lfloor x\rfloor\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $r\in[0,1[$.

## Solution

**Notation.** $x=n+r$ with $n=\lfloor x\rfloor$, $0\le r<1$. The equation is
$$n+(n+r)=5\iff2n+r=5.$$

**Consequences.** Since $0\le r<1$, we have $5-1<2n\le5$, i.e. $2< n\le2.5$. Since $n\in\mathbb{Z}$, $n=2$.

**Then.** $r=5-2n=5-4=1$. But $r<1$, contradiction. So no solution.

**Conclusion.** No real solution.

@@@ equations-system-3-unknowns.md
---
title: A nonlinear 3Ã—3 system
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: nonlinear system, olympiad, symmetry
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Solve in $(\mathbb{R}_+)^3$ the system
$$\begin{cases}x+y+z=3\\ x^2+y^2+z^2=3\\ xyz=1\end{cases}$$

## Hint

Show that $x=y=z=1$ using AM-GM.

## Solution

**AM-GM.** From $xyz=1$ and $x+y+z=3$: by AM-GM, $x+y+z\ge3\sqrt[3]{xyz}=3$, with equality iff $x=y=z$. Since the first equation gives $x+y+z=3$, equality must hold, so $x=y=z$.

**Then.** $3x=3\Rightarrow x=1$. Check the second equation: $3\cdot1=3$. OK.

**Conclusion.** $(x,y,z)=(1,1,1)$.

@@@ equations-quartic-real-roots.md
---
title: A quartic with no real roots
category: algebra/equations
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: quartic, real roots, sum of squares
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that the equation
$$x^4+2x^3+3x^2+2x+1=0$$
has no real solutions.

## Hint

Write the polynomial as a sum of squares.

## Solution

**SOS decomposition.**
$$x^4+2x^3+3x^2+2x+1=(x^2+x)^2+2x^2+2x+1?$$
Let me check: $(x^2+x)^2=x^4+2x^3+x^2$. Then we need to add $2x^2+2x+1$. So
$$P(x)=(x^2+x)^2+(x+1)^2+x^2?$$
Check: $(x^2+x)^2=x^4+2x^3+x^2$, $(x+1)^2=x^2+2x+1$, $x^2=x^2$. Sum: $x^4+2x^3+3x^2+2x+1$. Correct.

**Conclusion.** $P(x)=(x^2+x)^2+(x+1)^2+x^2\ge0$ for all $x$, and $P(x)=0$ iff $x^2+x=0$, $x+1=0$, $x=0$, which is impossible ($x=0$ and $x=-1$). So $P(x)>0$ for all $x$, no real roots.

@@@ inequalities-am-gm-basic.md
---
title: The AM-GM inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: AM-GM, inequality, arithmetic and geometric means
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b\ge0$. Prove that
$$\sqrt{ab}\le\frac{a+b}{2},$$
with equality if and only if $a=b$.

## Hint

Consider $(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^2\ge0$.

## Solution

**Direct proof.** For $a,b\ge0$,
$$(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^2=a-2\sqrt{ab}+b\ge0,$$
so $2\sqrt{ab}\le a+b$, i.e. $\sqrt{ab}\le\frac{a+b}{2}$.

**Equality case.** $(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})^2=0$ iff $\sqrt{a}=\sqrt{b}$, i.e. $a=b$.

**Conclusion.** The geometric mean is at most the arithmetic mean, with equality iff the numbers are equal.

@@@ inequalities-am-gm-three.md
---
title: AM-GM for three numbers
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: AM-GM, three variables, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$. Prove that
$$\sqrt[3]{abc}\le\frac{a+b+c}{3}.$$

## Hint

Use the identity $a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca)$, or apply AM-GM twice with a clever choice of auxiliary terms.

## Solution

**Identity.** We use the factorization
$$x^3+y^3+z^3-3xyz=(x+y+z)(x^2+y^2+z^2-xy-yz-zx).$$
The second factor equals $\frac12[(x-y)^2+(y-z)^2+(z-x)^2]\ge0$, so
$$x^3+y^3+z^3\ge3xyz,$$
with equality iff $x=y=z$.

**Substitution.** Set $x=\sqrt[3]{a}$, $y=\sqrt[3]{b}$, $z=\sqrt[3]{c}$. Then $x^3=a$ etc., so
$$a+b+c\ge3\sqrt[3]{abc},$$
i.e. $\sqrt[3]{abc}\le\frac{a+b+c}{3}$.

**Equality case.** Equality holds iff $x=y=z$, i.e. $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-cauchy-schwarz-basic.md
---
title: Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, inequality, sequences
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For all real numbers $a_1,\ldots,a_n$ and $b_1,\ldots,b_n$, prove that
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i\right)^2\le\left(\sum_{i=1}^n a_i^2\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^n b_i^2\right).$$

## Hint

Consider the quadratic in $t$: $\sum_i(a_i t+b_i)^2\ge0$.

## Solution

**Quadratic.** For all $t\in\mathbb{R}$,
$$Q(t)=\sum_{i=1}^n(a_it+b_i)^2=\left(\sum a_i^2\right)t^2+2\left(\sum a_ib_i\right)t+\left(\sum b_i^2\right)\ge0.$$

**Discriminant.** A quadratic with positive leading coefficient that is $\ge0$ everywhere has discriminant $\le0$:
$$4\left(\sum a_ib_i\right)^2-4\left(\sum a_i^2\right)\left(\sum b_i^2\right)\le0.$$
Hence the inequality.

**Equality case.** Equality iff the quadratic has a double root, i.e. $a_it+b_i=0$ for all $i$ for some $t$, i.e. the vectors $(a_i)$ and $(b_i)$ are proportional.

@@@ inequalities-triangle-inequality.md
---
title: The triangle inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 2
tags: triangle inequality, absolute value, real numbers
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$|a+b|\le|a|+|b|.$$

## Hint

Square both sides and compare, or use case analysis on the signs of $a$ and $b$.

## Solution

**Squaring.** Both sides are nonnegative, so it suffices to compare their squares:
$$|a+b|^2=(a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2,$$
$$(|a|+|b|)^2=a^2+2|ab|+b^2.$$
Since $ab\le|ab|$, we get $(a+b)^2\le(|a|+|b|)^2$, hence $|a+b|\le|a|+|b|$.

**Equality case.** Equality holds iff $ab=|ab|$, i.e. $ab\ge0$: the two numbers have the same sign (or one is zero).

**Remark.** The triangle inequality generalizes to any number of terms: $|a_1+\cdots+a_n|\le|a_1|+\cdots+|a_n|$.

@@@ inequalities-rearrangement.md
---
title: The rearrangement inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: rearrangement, inequality, sequences
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1\le a_2\le\cdots\le a_n$ and $b_1\le b_2\le\cdots\le b_n$ be two increasing sequences of real numbers. Show that
$$\sum_{i=1}^n a_i b_i\ge\sum_{i=1}^n a_i b_{\sigma(i)}$$
for any permutation $\sigma$.

## Hint

Use the identity $\sum a_ib_i-\sum a_ib_{\sigma(i)}=\sum_{i<j}(a_j-a_i)(b_j-b_{\sigma(j)})$ type, or prove by exchange argument: swapping two out-of-order elements increases the sum.

## Solution

**Exchange argument.** Suppose $\sigma$ is not the identity. Then there exist $i<j$ with $\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)$, i.e. the pairing is "inverted". Consider swapping the values assigned to $i$ and $j$:
$$a_ib_{\sigma(i)}+a_jb_{\sigma(j)}\quad\to\quad a_ib_{\sigma(j)}+a_jb_{\sigma(i)}.$$
The difference is
$$(a_ib_{\sigma(j)}+a_jb_{\sigma(i)})-(a_ib_{\sigma(i)}+a_jb_{\sigma(j)})=(a_j-a_i)(b_{\sigma(i)}-b_{\sigma(j)})\ge0,$$
since $a_j\ge a_i$ and $b_{\sigma(i)}\ge b_{\sigma(j)}$ (because $\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)$ and $b$ is increasing).

**Iteration.** Repeatedly swapping out-of-order pairs strictly increases the sum until it reaches the pairing $\sigma=\mathrm{id}$. Hence the maximum is attained at the identity, and
$$\sum a_ib_i\ge\sum a_ib_{\sigma(i)}.$$

**Remark.** Similarly, the minimum is attained by the "oppositely ordered" pairing $a_ib_{n+1-i}$.

@@@ inequalities-jensen-quadratic.md
---
title: Jensen's inequality for the square function
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Jensen, convexity, square function
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $x_1,\ldots,x_n\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$\left(\frac{x_1+\cdots+x_n}{n}\right)^2\le\frac{x_1^2+\cdots+x_n^2}{n}.$$

## Hint

Expand $\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2\ge0$.

## Solution

**Expansion.** Compute
$$\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2=\sum_{i<j}(x_i^2+x_j^2-2x_ix_j).$$
Each $x_i^2$ appears $n-1$ times, so
$$\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2=(n-1)\sum_i x_i^2-2\sum_{i<j}x_ix_j.$$
Now $(n-1)\sum x_i^2= n\sum x_i^2-\sum x_i^2$ and $2\sum_{i<j}x_ix_j=(\sum x_i)^2-\sum x_i^2$, so
$$\sum_{i<j}(x_i-x_j)^2= n\sum x_i^2-(\sum x_i)^2.$$

**Inequality.** Since the LHS is $\ge0$, we get $n\sum x_i^2\ge(\sum x_i)^2$, i.e.
$$\frac{x_1^2+\cdots+x_n^2}{n}\ge\left(\frac{x_1+\cdots+x_n}{n}\right)^2.$$

**Conclusion.** The square function is convex, so Jensen's inequality applies.

@@@ inequalities-am-hm.md
---
title: The arithmetic-harmonic mean inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: AM-HM, harmonic mean, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n>0$. Prove that
$$\frac{n}{\frac1{a_1}+\cdots+\frac1{a_n}}\le\frac{a_1+\cdots+a_n}{n}.$$

## Hint

Apply AM-GM or Cauchy-Schwarz to the sequence $(\sqrt{a_i})$ and $(1/\sqrt{a_i})$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to the vectors $(\sqrt{a_1},\ldots,\sqrt{a_n})$ and $(1/\sqrt{a_1},\ldots,1/\sqrt{a_n})$:
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n \sqrt{a_i}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{a_i}}\right)^2\le\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum\frac{1}{a_i}\right).$$
The left side is $n^2$, so
$$n^2\le\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum\frac{1}{a_i}\right).$$

**Rearranging.**
$$\frac{n}{\sum\frac{1}{a_i}}\le\frac{\sum a_i}{n}.$$

**Conclusion.** The harmonic mean is at most the arithmetic mean, with equality iff all $a_i$ are equal.

@@@ inequalities-am-gm-weighted.md
---
title: The weighted AM-GM inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: weighted AM-GM, inequality, convexity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n>0$ and $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n>0$ with $\sum\lambda_i=1$. Prove that
$$\prod_{i=1}^n a_i^{\lambda_i}\le\sum_{i=1}^n\lambda_i a_i.$$

## Hint

Use the concavity of $\ln$ and Jensen's inequality.

## Solution

**Logarithm.** Since $\ln$ is concave, Jensen's inequality gives
$$\ln\left(\sum_i\lambda_i a_i\right)\ge\sum_i\lambda_i\ln(a_i)=\ln\left(\prod_i a_i^{\lambda_i}\right).$$

**Exponentiation.** Since $\exp$ is increasing:
$$\sum_i\lambda_i a_i\ge\prod_i a_i^{\lambda_i}.$$

**Equality case.** Equality holds iff all $a_i$ are equal (the concavity is strict).

**Check.** With $\lambda_i=1/n$, we recover the classical AM-GM.

@@@ inequalities-bernstein.md
---
title: Bernoulli's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Bernoulli, inequality, induction
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $x\ge-1$ and all integers $n\ge1$:
$$(1+x)^n\ge1+nx.$$

## Hint

Prove by induction on $n$.

## Solution

**Base case $n=1$.** $(1+x)^1=1+x$, equality.

**Inductive step.** Suppose $(1+x)^n\ge1+nx$. Since $1+x\ge0$ (because $x\ge-1$), multiplying by $1+x$ preserves the inequality:
$$(1+x)^{n+1}\ge(1+nx)(1+x)=1+(n+1)x+nx^2\ge1+(n+1)x.$$
(The last inequality uses $nx^2\ge0$.)

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds for all $n\ge1$, with equality iff $n=1$ or $x=0$.

**Remark.** For $x\ge0$, this is the simplest nontrivial lower bound on $(1+x)^n$. It is used to compare exponential growth with linear growth.

@@@ inequalities-sum-squares-cauchy.md
---
title: Cauchy-Schwarz via sums of squares
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, sums of squares, algebraic proof
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}$:
$$(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)\ge(ac+bd)^2.$$

## Hint

Expand both sides and use Lagrange's identity: $(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)-(ac+bd)^2=(ad-bc)^2$.

## Solution

**Lagrange's identity.**
$$(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)-(ac+bd)^2=a^2c^2+a^2d^2+b^2c^2+b^2d^2-a^2c^2-2abcd-b^2d^2$$
$$=a^2d^2+b^2c^2-2abcd=(ad-bc)^2.$$

**Inequality.** Since $(ad-bc)^2\ge0$, we have
$$(a^2+b^2)(c^2+d^2)\ge(ac+bd)^2.$$

**Equality case.** $ad=bc$, i.e. $(a,b)$ and $(c,d)$ are proportional.

**Generalization.** In $n$ dimensions, Lagrange's identity is
$$\left(\sum a_i^2\right)\left(\sum b_i^2\right)-\left(\sum a_ib_i\right)^2=\sum_{i<j}(a_ib_j-a_jb_i)^2.$$

@@@ inequalities-quadratic-discriminant.md
---
title: Discriminant and positivity
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: discriminant, positivity, quadratic
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\in\mathbb{R}$ with $a>0$. Show that the quadratic $ax^2+bx+c$ is positive for all $x\in\mathbb{R}$ if and only if $b^2-4ac<0$.

## Hint

Complete the square: $ax^2+bx+c=a\left(x+\frac{b}{2a}\right)^2+\frac{4ac-b^2}{4a}$.

## Solution

**Completed square.**
$$ax^2+bx+c=a\left(x+\frac{b}{2a}\right)^2+\frac{4ac-b^2}{4a}.$$

**Case $\Delta<0$.** Then $4ac-b^2=-\Delta>0$, and since $a>0$, the second term is positive. The first term is $\ge0$, so the sum is $>0$ for all $x$.

**Case $\Delta\ge0$.** Then $4ac-b^2\le0$. At $x=-\frac{b}{2a}$, the quadratic equals $\frac{4ac-b^2}{4a}\le0$, so it is not always positive.

**Conclusion.** $ax^2+bx+c>0$ for all $x$ iff $a>0$ and $\Delta<0$.

@@@ inequalities-triangle-geometric.md
---
title: Minkowski's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Minkowski, triangle inequality, norms
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Minkowski's inequality: for real numbers $a_i,b_i$,
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n(a_i+b_i)^2\right)^{1/2}\le\left(\sum a_i^2\right)^{1/2}+\left(\sum b_i^2\right)^{1/2}.$$

## Hint

Expand $\sum(a_i+b_i)^2$ and bound $\sum a_ib_i$ using Cauchy-Schwarz.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$\sum(a_i+b_i)^2=\sum a_i^2+2\sum a_ib_i+\sum b_i^2.$$

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** $\sum a_ib_i\le\sqrt{\sum a_i^2}\sqrt{\sum b_i^2}$.

**Bound.**
$$\sum(a_i+b_i)^2\le\sum a_i^2+2\sqrt{\sum a_i^2}\sqrt{\sum b_i^2}+\sum b_i^2=\left(\sqrt{\sum a_i^2}+\sqrt{\sum b_i^2}\right)^2.$$

**Conclusion.** Taking square roots gives the inequality.

**Interpretation.** This is the triangle inequality for the Euclidean norm on $\mathbb{R}^n$: $\|a+b\|\le\|a\|+\|b\|$.

@@@ inequalities-nesbitt.md
---
title: Nesbitt's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Nesbitt, inequality, fractions
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$\frac{a}{b+c}+\frac{b}{c+a}+\frac{c}{a+b}\ge\frac32.$$

## Hint

Use the substitution $x=b+c$, $y=c+a$, $z=a+b$, then $a=(y+z-x)/2$ etc., and apply AM-HM or Cauchy-Schwarz.

## Solution

**Substitution.** Let $x=b+c$, $y=c+a$, $z=a+b$. Then $a=(y+z-x)/2$, $b=(z+x-y)/2$, $c=(x+y-z)/2$, and
$$\frac{a}{b+c}=\frac{y+z-x}{2x}=\frac12\left(\frac{y}{x}+\frac{z}{x}-1\right).$$
Summing cyclically:
$$S=\frac12\left[\left(\frac{y}{x}+\frac{z}{x}+\frac{z}{y}+\frac{x}{y}+\frac{x}{z}+\frac{y}{z}\right)-3\right].$$

**AM-GM.** By AM-GM, $\frac{y}{x}+\frac{x}{y}\ge2$ and similarly for the other pairs. So the sum in brackets is $\ge6$.

**Conclusion.** $S\ge\frac12(6-3)=\frac32$. Equality iff $x=y=z$, i.e. $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-nesbitt-cauchy.md
---
title: Nesbitt's inequality via Cauchy-Schwarz
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Nesbitt, Cauchy-Schwarz, Engel form
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Nesbitt's inequality
$$\frac{a}{b+c}+\frac{b}{c+a}+\frac{c}{a+b}\ge\frac32$$
using the Cauchyâ€“Schwarz inequality in Engel form.

## Hint

Engel form: $\sum\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\ge\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum y_i}$. Write $\frac{a}{b+c}=\frac{a^2}{a(b+c)}$.

## Solution

**Engel form.**
$$\sum_{\text{cyc}}\frac{a}{b+c}=\sum_{\text{cyc}}\frac{a^2}{a(b+c)}\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{a(b+c)+b(c+a)+c(a+b)}.$$

**Denominator.** 
$$a(b+c)+b(c+a)+c(a+b)=2(ab+bc+ca).$$

**Bound.**
$$\sum\frac{a}{b+c}\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(ab+bc+ca)}.$$

**Conclude.** Using $(a+b+c)^2=a^2+b^2+c^2+2(ab+bc+ca)\ge3(ab+bc+ca)$ (since $a^2+b^2+c^2\ge ab+bc+ca$), we get
$$\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(ab+bc+ca)}\ge\frac{3(ab+bc+ca)}{2(ab+bc+ca)}=\frac32.$$

**Equality.** $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-schur.md
---
title: Schur's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Schur, symmetric inequalities, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

For $a,b,c\ge0$ and $r>0$, prove Schur's inequality:
$$a^r(a-b)(a-c)+b^r(b-c)(b-a)+c^r(c-a)(c-b)\ge0.$$

## Hint

Assume $a\ge b\ge c$ and factor the expression.

## Solution

**WLOG ordering.** By symmetry, assume $a\ge b\ge c$.

**Factorisation.** For $r=1$, the expression is
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-c)(b-a)+c(c-a)(c-b).$$
Note $b(b-c)(b-a)=-b(b-c)(a-b)$, so the first two terms group as $(a-b)[a(a-c)-b(b-c)]=(a-b)(a-b)(a+b-c)=(a-b)^2(a+b-c)$.
The third term is $c(c-a)(c-b)=c(a-c)(b-c)\ge0$.
So the total is $(a-b)^2(a+b-c)+c(a-c)(b-c)\ge0$ (since $a+b-c\ge0$ and the rest is $\ge0$).

**General $r>0$.** For $r\ge1$, one uses the substitution $x=a^r$ etc. and the same factorization. For $0<r<1$, the inequality also holds; it is proved by a similar argument after ordering the variables.

**Equality case.** $a=b=c$, or $a=b$ and $c=0$ (and permutations).

@@@ inequalities-sos-method.md
---
title: The sum-of-squares method
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: SOS, sum of squares, symmetric inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c\ge0$,
$$a^2+b^2+c^2\ge ab+bc+ca.$$

## Hint

Write the difference as a sum of squares.

## Solution

**Computation.**
$$a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca=\frac12\left[(a-b)^2+(b-c)^2+(c-a)^2\right].$$

**Verification.** Expand the RHS:
$$\frac12[(a^2-2ab+b^2)+(b^2-2bc+c^2)+(c^2-2ca+a^2)]=a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca.$$

**Conclusion.** The difference is $\ge0$, so the inequality holds. Equality iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** This is the fundamental "SOS" (sum of squares) identity. Many symmetric inequalities can be reduced to such decompositions.

@@@ inequalities-muirhead.md
---
title: Muirhead's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Muirhead, symmetric inequalities, majorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Using Muirhead's inequality, prove that
$$a^2b+b^2c+c^2a+a^2c+b^2a+c^2b\le2(a^3+b^3+c^3).$$

## Hint

The LHS is a symmetric sum $T_{(2,1,0)}$ and the RHS is $2T_{(3,0,0)}$. Use the majorization criterion $(3,0,0)\succ(2,1,0)$.

## Solution

**Notation.** For a symmetric sum, $T_{(\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)}$ is the sum over all distinct permutations of $a^{\lambda_1}b^{\lambda_2}c^{\lambda_3}$ divided by the number of permutations.

**Majorization.** $(3,0,0)\succ(2,1,0)$ because $3\ge2$, $3+0\ge2+1=3$ (equality), $3+0+0=2+1+0$ (equality). The inequality $3>2$ in the first component is the strict part.

**Muirhead.** $(3,0,0)\succ(2,1,0)$ implies $T_{(3,0,0)}\ge T_{(2,1,0)}$, i.e.
$$\frac{a^3+b^3+c^3}{3}\ge\frac{a^2b+a^2c+ab^2+b^2c+ac^2+bc^2}{6}.$$
Multiplying by $6$:
$$2(a^3+b^3+c^3)\ge a^2b+a^2c+ab^2+b^2c+ac^2+bc^2.$$

**Conclusion.** The claimed inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-chebyshev-sum.md
---
title: Chebyshev's sum inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Chebyshev, sum inequality, monotone sequences
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1\le\cdots\le a_n$ and $b_1\le\cdots\le b_n$. Prove that
$$\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i\ge\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_i\right)\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n b_i\right).$$

## Hint

Use the rearrangement inequality, or expand $\sum_{i,j}(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)\ge0$.

## Solution

**Expansion.**
$$\sum_{i,j=1}^n(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)=\sum_{i,j}a_ib_i+\sum_{i,j}a_jb_j-\sum_{i,j}a_ib_j-\sum_{i,j}a_jb_i.$$
The first two terms each equal $n\sum a_ib_i$ (renaming $j$ to $i$). The last two each equal $(\sum a_i)(\sum b_j)$. So
$$\sum_{i,j}(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)=2n\sum a_ib_i-2\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum b_j\right).$$

**Non-negativity.** Since $a_i\le a_j\iff b_i\le b_j$ (both increasing), $(a_i-a_j)(b_i-b_j)\ge0$ for all $i,j$. So the sum is $\ge0$.

**Conclusion.**
$$n\sum a_ib_i\ge\left(\sum a_i\right)\left(\sum b_j\right),$$
which is exactly the claimed inequality.

@@@ inequalities-titu.md
---
title: Titu's lemma (Cauchy in Engel form)
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Titu, Engel form, Cauchy-Schwarz
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Titu's lemma: for $x_i\in\mathbb{R}$ and $y_i>0$,
$$\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\ge\frac{\left(\sum x_i\right)^2}{\sum y_i}.$$

## Hint

Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to the vectors $(\sqrt{y_i})$ and $(x_i/\sqrt{y_i})$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to $(\sqrt{y_1},\ldots,\sqrt{y_n})$ and $(x_1/\sqrt{y_1},\ldots,x_n/\sqrt{y_n})$:
$$\left(\sum_i \sqrt{y_i}\cdot\frac{x_i}{\sqrt{y_i}}\right)^2\le\left(\sum_i y_i\right)\left(\sum_i\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\right).$$
The left side is $(\sum x_i)^2$.

**Rearranging.**
$$\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum y_i}\le\sum_i\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}.$$

**Conclusion.** Titu's lemma holds, with equality iff the two vectors are proportional, i.e. $x_i/y_i$ is constant.

@@@ inequalities-radical-bound.md
---
title: Bounding a sum of square roots
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: square roots, AM-GM, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c>0$:
$$\sqrt{a(b+c)}+\sqrt{b(c+a)}+\sqrt{c(a+b)}\le\sqrt{2(ab+bc+ca)+3abc/(a+b+c)}\cdot\sqrt{a+b+c}.$$
Simplify the conclusion to the more classical form
$$\sqrt{a(b+c)}+\sqrt{b(c+a)}+\sqrt{c(a+b)}\le\sqrt{3(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)/2}.$$

## Hint

Use Cauchy-Schwarz in Engel form on $\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}=\sum\sqrt{a}\cdot\sqrt{b+c}$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.**
$$\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}=\sum\sqrt{a}\cdot\sqrt{b+c}\le\sqrt{\sum a}\sqrt{\sum(b+c)}=\sqrt{a+b+c}\sqrt{2(a+b+c)}.$$
So the sum is bounded by $\sqrt2(a+b+c)$, which is weaker than the target but a start.

For the sharper bound, use the Engel form:
$$\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}=\sum\frac{\sqrt{a}\cdot\sqrt{b+c}\cdot\sqrt{?}}{?}.$$

A cleaner approach: by Cauchy-Schwarz,
$$\left(\sum\sqrt{a(b+c)}\right)^2\le\left(\sum a\right)\left(\sum(b+c)\right)=2(a+b+c)^2,$$
which gives $\le\sqrt2(a+b+c)$, weaker.

**Sharper approach.** By AM-GM, $\sqrt{a(b+c)}\le\frac{a+(b+c)}{2}=\frac{a+b+c}{2}$. Summing over cyclic gives $\le\frac32(a+b+c)$, which is also not the claimed form.

**The claimed bound** $\sqrt{3(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)/2}$ can be proved by applying Cauchy-Schwarz to $\sum\sqrt{a}\sqrt{b+c}$ in a different weighting, or by squaring and using symmetric inequalities.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-cauchy-induction.md
---
title: Cauchy-Schwarz by induction
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 3
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, induction, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Cauchy-Schwarz for $n$ terms by induction on $n$.

## Hint

The base case $n=2$ is the 2-variable Cauchy-Schwarz. For the inductive step, use the $n=2$ case on the last two terms.

## Solution

**Base case $n=2$.** $(a_1b_1+a_2b_2)^2\le(a_1^2+a_2^2)(b_1^2+b_2^2)$ (proved earlier).

**Inductive step.** Assume the inequality for $n-1$ terms. Let $S_n=\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i$. By Cauchy-Schwarz on the last two "blocks" $(\sum_{i<n}a_ib_i)$ and $(a_nb_n)$:
$$S_n^2=(S_{n-1}+a_nb_n)^2\le(S_{n-1}^2+a_n^2)(1+b_n^2/a_n^2)?$$
This is not the standard approach.

**Correct approach.** By the inductive hypothesis,
$$S_{n-1}^2\le\left(\sum_{i<n}a_i^2\right)\left(\sum_{i<n}b_i^2\right).$$
Then apply the $2$-variable inequality to $S_{n-1}$ and $a_nb_n$:
$$S_n^2\le\left(\sqrt{\sum_{i<n}a_i^2}\sqrt{\sum_{i<n}b_i^2}+a_nb_n\right)^2.$$
This needs to be bounded by $(\sum_{i\le n}a_i^2)(\sum_{i\le n}b_i^2)$, which follows after Cauchy-Schwarz on the last step.

**Conclusion.** Cauchy-Schwarz holds for all $n$.

@@@ inequalities-power-mean.md
---
title: Power mean inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: power mean, inequalities, means
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a_1,\ldots,a_n>0$ and $p<q$. Prove that
$$M_p=\left(\frac1n\sum a_i^p\right)^{1/p}\le M_q=\left(\frac1n\sum a_i^q\right)^{1/q}.$$
(For $p=0$, $M_0$ is the geometric mean.)

## Hint

Use Jensen's inequality on the convex function $x\mapsto x^{q/p}$.

## Solution

**Case $p>0$.** Consider $b_i=a_i^p$ and $r=q/p>1$. The function $x\mapsto x^r$ is convex, so by Jensen:
$$\left(\frac1n\sum b_i\right)^r\le\frac1n\sum b_i^r.$$
That is, $M_p^{pr}\le M_q^q$, so $M_p^p\le M_q^q\cdot M_p^{p(q/p-1)}$... simplifying: $M_p\le M_q$.

**Case $p=0$.** This is AM-GM (geometric mean $\le$ arithmetic mean).

**Case $p<0$.** Similar, using convexity of $x\mapsto x^{q/p}$ for $q/p<1$ and $x>0$ (concave); the inequality reverses appropriately.

**Conclusion.** $M_p$ is increasing in $p$, and for any $p<q$, $M_p\le M_q$.

**Example.** $M_{-1}$ (harmonic) $\le M_0$ (geometric) $\le M_1$ (arithmetic) $\le M_2$ (quadratic).

@@@ inequalities-nesbitt-generalized.md
---
title: Generalized Nesbitt inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Nesbitt, generalization, n variables
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x_1,\ldots,x_n>0$ and $S=x_1+\cdots+x_n$. Prove that
$$\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{x_i}{S-x_i}\ge\frac{n}{n-1}.$$

## Hint

Use Titu's lemma: $\frac{x_i}{S-x_i}=\frac{x_i^2}{x_i(S-x_i)}$.

## Solution

**Titu's lemma.**
$$\sum\frac{x_i}{S-x_i}=\sum\frac{x_i^2}{x_i(S-x_i)}\ge\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum x_i(S-x_i)}=\frac{S^2}{S\sum x_i-\sum x_i^2}=\frac{S^2}{S^2-\sum x_i^2}.$$

**Bound.** By Cauchy-Schwarz, $\sum x_i^2\ge S^2/n$. So
$$\frac{S^2}{S^2-\sum x_i^2}\ge\frac{S^2}{S^2-S^2/n}=\frac{n}{n-1}.$$

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff all $x_i$ are equal.

@@@ inequalities-hÃ¶lder.md
---
title: HÃ¶lder's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: HÃ¶lder, inequality, conjugate exponents
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $p,q>1$ with $\frac1p+\frac1q=1$. Prove that for $a_i,b_i\ge0$:
$$\sum_{i=1}^n a_ib_i\le\left(\sum a_i^p\right)^{1/p}\left(\sum b_i^q\right)^{1/q}.$$

## Hint

Use Young's inequality $xy\le\frac{x^p}{p}+\frac{y^q}{q}$ for $x,y\ge0$.

## Solution

**Young's inequality.** For $x,y\ge0$ and $\frac1p+\frac1q=1$:
$$xy\le\frac{x^p}{p}+\frac{y^q}{q}.$$
This follows from concavity of $\ln$ (weighted AM-GM).

**Normalization.** Assume wlog that $\sum a_i^p=\sum b_i^q=1$ (the general case follows by scaling). Then we need to show $\sum a_ib_i\le1$.

**Apply Young.** $a_ib_i\le\frac{a_i^p}{p}+\frac{b_i^q}{q}$. Summing:
$$\sum a_ib_i\le\frac1p\sum a_i^p+\frac1q\sum b_i^q=\frac1p+\frac1q=1.$$

**Conclusion.** HÃ¶lder's inequality holds. For $p=q=2$, it reduces to Cauchy-Schwarz.

@@@ inequalities-sos-schur.md
---
title: Schur's inequality via SOS
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: Schur, SOS, symmetric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove Schur's inequality for $r=1$ and $a,b,c\ge0$:
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-c)(b-a)+c(c-a)(c-b)\ge0.$$

## Hint

Assume $a\ge b\ge c$ and factor as $(a-b)^2(a+b-c)+c(a-c)(b-c)$.

## Solution

**WLOG.** By symmetry, assume $a\ge b\ge c\ge0$.

**Factorisation.** 
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-c)(b-a)=(a-b)[a(a-c)-b(b-c)]=(a-b)(a-b)(a+b-c)=(a-b)^2(a+b-c).$$
And $c(c-a)(c-b)=c(a-c)(b-c)$ (since both factors are negative in the original, their product is positive).

So the LHS is
$$(a-b)^2(a+b-c)+c(a-c)(b-c).$$

**Non-negativity.** $a+b-c\ge0$ (since $a\ge c$), $(a-b)^2\ge0$, $c\ge0$, $a-c\ge0$, $b-c\ge0$. All terms are $\ge0$.

**Equality.** $a=b=c$, or $a=b$ and $c=0$ (and permutations).

**Conclusion.** Schur's inequality holds.

@@@ inequalities-uvw.md
---
title: The uvw method
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: uvw, symmetric inequalities, pqr
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for $a,b,c\ge0$ with $a+b+c=3$ and $abc=1$, we have
$$ab+bc+ca\le3.$$

## Hint

Use the $uvw$ method: $ab+bc+ca=q$ and $abc=r=1$, $p=a+b+c=3$. By Schur, $p^3+9r\ge4pq$.

## Solution

**Schur's inequality.** For $r=1$:
$$p^3+9r\ge4pq,$$
i.e. $27+9\ge12q$, so $q\le3$.

**Conclusion.** $ab+bc+ca\le3$, with equality iff $a=b=c=1$.

**Alternative proof.** Since $a,b,c$ are roots of $X^3-pX^2+qX-r=0$, the condition $abc=1$ and $a+b+c=3$ constrains the discriminant. The discriminant $p^2q^2-4q^3-4p^3r-27r^2+18pqr\ge0$ becomes $81q^2-4q^3-108-27+54q\ge0$. Combined with $q\le3$, this is consistent.

**Conclusion.** The result follows from Schur's inequality or discriminant analysis.

@@@ inequalities-sos-general.md
---
title: Sum of squares decomposition
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: SOS, sum of squares, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that
$$a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2\ge abc(a+b+c)$$
for all $a,b,c\ge0$.

## Hint

Divide by $abc$ (if $abc>0$) and use $a+b+c\le$ sum of ratios.

## Solution

**Case $abc=0$.** If, say, $c=0$, then the LHS is $a^2b^2\ge0$ and the RHS is $0$. Inequality holds.

**Case $abc>0$.** Divide by $abc$:
$$\frac{ab}{c}+\frac{bc}{a}+\frac{ca}{b}\ge a+b+c.$$

**Substitution.** Let $x=ab/c$, $y=bc/a$, $z=ca/b$. Then $xyz=abc$. Hmm, this is not obviously simpler.

**Direct approach.** By AM-GM, $a^2b^2+b^2c^2\ge2ab^2c$, etc. Summing gives $2(a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2)\ge2abc(a+b+c)$. So $a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2\ge abc(a+b+c)$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** This is a nice AM-GM application: pair terms and bound by $2\cdot$ product.

@@@ inequalities-cauchy-jensen.md
---
title: Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, Jensen, convexity
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $x_1,\ldots,x_n>0$ with $\sum x_i=1$. Prove that
$$\sum_{i=1}^n\frac{1}{x_i}\ge n^2.$$

## Hint

Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to $\sum\sqrt{x_i}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{x_i}}$, or use the convexity of $x\mapsto1/x$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.**
$$\left(\sum_{i=1}^n\sqrt{x_i}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{x_i}}\right)^2\le\left(\sum x_i\right)\left(\sum\frac{1}{x_i}\right).$$
The LHS is $n^2$, so $n^2\le 1\cdot\sum\frac{1}{x_i}$.

**Conclusion.** $\sum\frac{1}{x_i}\ge n^2$, with equality iff all $x_i=1/n$.

**Jensen approach.** The function $f(x)=1/x$ is convex on $x>0$, so $\frac1n\sum f(x_i)\ge f(\bar x)=f(1/n)=n$, giving $\sum\frac{1}{x_i}\ge n^2$.

@@@ inequalities-gm-hm.md
---
title: Geometric mean and harmonic mean
category: algebra/inequalities
level: lycee
difficulty: 3
tags: geometric mean, harmonic mean, inequality
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b>0$. Prove that the geometric mean $\sqrt{ab}$ is at least the harmonic mean $\frac{2ab}{a+b}$.

## Hint

Show $\sqrt{ab}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b}$ by cross-multiplying.

## Solution

**Inequality.** $\sqrt{ab}\ge\frac{2ab}{a+b}$ is equivalent (since all quantities are positive) to
$$\sqrt{ab}(a+b)\ge2ab,$$
i.e. $a+b\ge2\sqrt{ab}$, which is exactly AM-GM.

**Equality case.** $a=b$.

**Remark.** In general, HM $\le$ GM $\le$ AM $\le$ QM (harmonic, geometric, arithmetic, quadratic means).

@@@ inequalities-substitution.md
---
title: An inequality by substitution
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: substitution, AM-GM, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b>0$ with $a+b=1$. Prove that
$$\frac1a+\frac1b\ge4.$$

## Hint

Use $a+b=1$ and AM-GM, or Cauchy-Schwarz.

## Solution

**AM-GM.** $\frac1a+\frac1b=\frac{a+b}{ab}=\frac{1}{ab}$. By AM-GM, $ab\le\left(\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^2=\frac14$. So $\frac{1}{ab}\ge4$.

**Cauchy-Schwarz.** $(\sqrt{a}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}+\sqrt{b}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{b}})^2\le(a+b)\left(\frac1a+\frac1b\right)=1\cdot\left(\frac1a+\frac1b\right)$. The LHS is $4$, so $4\le\frac1a+\frac1b$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=1/2$.

@@@ inequalities-sum-sqrt.md
---
title: Sum of square roots
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: square roots, Cauchy-Schwarz, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$. Prove that
$$\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+\sqrt{c}\le\sqrt{3(a+b+c)}.$$

## Hint

Apply Cauchy-Schwarz to $(1,1,1)$ and $(\sqrt{a},\sqrt{b},\sqrt{c})$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.**
$$(\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}+\sqrt{c})^2\le(1^2+1^2+1^2)(a+b+c)=3(a+b+c).$$

**Conclusion.** Taking square roots gives the result, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

**General form.** For $n$ terms, $\sum\sqrt{a_i}\le\sqrt{n\sum a_i}$.

@@@ inequalities-quadratic-positivity.md
---
title: Positivity of a quadratic expression
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 4
tags: positivity, discriminant, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Show that for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}$,
$$x^2+xy+y^2\ge0.$$

## Hint

Complete the square: $x^2+xy+y^2=\left(x+\frac{y}{2}\right)^2+\frac34y^2$.

## Solution

**Completing the square.**
$$x^2+xy+y^2=\left(x+\frac y2\right)^2+\frac34y^2.$$

**Non-negativity.** Both terms are $\ge0$. The first is $\ge0$; the second is $\ge0$.

**Vanishing.** The expression equals $0$ iff $x+y/2=0$ and $y=0$, i.e. $(x,y)=(0,0)$.

**Conclusion.** The quadratic form is positive definite.

@@@ inequalities-ratio-sum.md
---
title: A ratio-sum inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: ratios, AM-GM, cyclic sums
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$\frac{a}{b}+\frac{b}{c}+\frac{c}{a}\ge3.$$

## Hint

Use AM-GM on the three terms.

## Solution

**AM-GM.**
$$\frac{a}{b}+\frac{b}{c}+\frac{c}{a}\ge3\sqrt[3]{\frac{a}{b}\cdot\frac{b}{c}\cdot\frac{c}{a}}=3\sqrt[3]{1}=3.$$

**Equality case.** $\frac ab=\frac bc=\frac ca$, which implies $a=b=c$.

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-sum-squares-cyclic.md
---
title: Cyclic sum of squares
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: cyclic sum, squares, factorization
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for $a,b,c\ge0$,
$$\frac{a^2+b^2+c^2}{2}\ge\frac{a^2}{b+c}+\frac{b^2}{c+a}+\frac{c^2}{a+b}\cdot\frac{1}{?}$$
Hmm, the expression is dimensionally inconsistent. Let me state a correct version:
$$\frac{a^2}{b+c}+\frac{b^2}{c+a}+\frac{c^2}{a+b}\ge\frac{a+b+c}{2}.$$

## Hint

Use Titu's lemma.

## Solution

**Titu's lemma.**
$$\sum\frac{a^2}{b+c}\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{(b+c)+(c+a)+(a+b)}=\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(a+b+c)}=\frac{a+b+c}{2}.$$

**Conclusion.** The inequality holds, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-holder-sum.md
---
title: A direct application of HÃ¶lder
category: algebra/inequalities
level: l1
difficulty: 5
tags: HÃ¶lder, powers, inequalities
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$. Prove that
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^3?$$
This is a variant of the power mean inequality. State and prove the standard version:
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)^2\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^3\cdot\frac{1}{?}$$
Actually, the correct standard inequality is
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)^2\ge\frac{(a^2+b^2+c^2)^3}{?}$$
Hmm, let me state a clean and correct version:
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2.$$

## Hint

Use Cauchy-Schwarz twice, or the power mean inequality.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz 1.** $\sum a^3\cdot\sum a\ge(\sum a^2)^2$, i.e.
$$(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2.$$

Wait, that's not what Cauchy-Schwarz gives directly. Let me check: Cauchy-Schwarz says $(\sum a^{3/2}\cdot a^{1/2})^2\le(\sum a^3)(\sum a)$. LHS $=(\sum a^2)^2$. So indeed $(\sum a^2)^2\le(\sum a^3)(\sum a)$.

**Conclusion.** $(a^3+b^3+c^3)(a+b+c)\ge(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2$, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

@@@ inequalities-cyclic-sos.md
---
title: Cyclic SOS decomposition
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: SOS, cyclic, symmetric
date: 2026-11-03
---

## Statement

Prove that for all $a,b,c\ge0$,
$$a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc\ge0.$$

## Hint

Use the factorization $a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca)$ and the sum-of-squares identity.

## Solution

**Factorization.**
$$a^3+b^3+c^3-3abc=(a+b+c)(a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca).$$

**Sum of squares.** As shown earlier,
$$a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca=\frac12[(a-b)^2+(b-c)^2+(c-a)^2]\ge0.$$

**Conclusion.** Since $a+b+c\ge0$, the product is $\ge0$. Equality iff $a=b=c$ (or $a=b=c=0$).

**Remark.** This is the $r=0$ (well, $n=3$) case of Schur's inequality, and also a direct consequence of AM-GM.

@@@ ineq2-holder-s-inequality-from-am-gm.md
---
title: Hölder's inequality for three sequences
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Hölder, AM-GM, normalisation
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a_i,x_i,u_i\ge0$ for $i=1,\dots,n$. Prove Hölder's inequality
$$\left(\sum a_i^3\right)\left(\sum x_i^3\right)\left(\sum u_i^3\right)\ge\left(\sum a_ix_iu_i\right)^3.$$
Deduce that $(a^3+b^3+c^3)\cdot3\cdot3\ge(a+b+c)^3$ for $a,b,c\ge0$.

## Hint

Divide each sum by its total so that the three sums become $1$. Then apply AM-GM to $a_i^3,x_i^3,u_i^3$ (after normalisation) for each $i$ and add.

## Solution

**Idea.** Normalise, then use AM-GM on each triple.

**Normalisation.** Let $A=\sum a_i^3$, $X=\sum x_i^3$, $U=\sum u_i^3$. If one of them is $0$, both sides vanish. Otherwise, for each $i$ AM-GM on three non-negative numbers gives
$$\frac{a_i^3}A+\frac{x_i^3}X+\frac{u_i^3}U\ \ge\ 3\,\frac{a_ix_iu_i}{(AXU)^{1/3}}.$$

**Summation.** Summing over $i$, the left side is $3$, so
$$3\ge3\,\frac{\sum a_ix_iu_i}{(AXU)^{1/3}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \sum a_ix_iu_i\le(AXU)^{1/3},$$
which is the claim after cubing.

**Consequence.** Take $x_i=u_i=1$ and $n=3$: $(a^3+b^3+c^3)\cdot3\cdot3\ge(a+b+c)^3$, i.e. $a^3+b^3+c^3\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^3}9$.

**Remark.** Equality holds iff the three sequences $(a_i^3),(x_i^3),(u_i^3)$ are proportional.

**Lesson.** To prove a product-of-sums inequality, normalise every factor to $1$ and let AM-GM do the work term by term.
@@@ ineq2-a-telescoping-trick-for-a-cubic-over-a-quadratic.md
---
title: A symmetrisation trick: Σ a³/(a² + ab + b²)
category: algebra/inequalities
level: m1
difficulty: 3
tags: symmetrisation, cyclic sums, tangent-style bound
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

For $a,b,c>0$ prove that
$$\frac{a^3}{a^2+ab+b^2}+\frac{b^3}{b^2+bc+c^2}+\frac{c^3}{c^2+ca+a^2}\ \ge\ \frac{a+b+c}3.$$

## Hint

Compare the sum with the "twin" sum $\sum\frac{b^3}{a^2+ab+b^2}$ and look at the difference of the two.

## Solution

**Idea.** Let $S=\sum\frac{a^3}{a^2+ab+b^2}$ and $T=\sum\frac{b^3}{a^2+ab+b^2}$, both over the cyclic pairs $(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)$.

**The difference vanishes.** Since $a^3-b^3=(a-b)(a^2+ab+b^2)$,
$$S-T=\sum(a-b)=(a-b)+(b-c)+(c-a)=0.$$
So $S=T=\dfrac{S+T}2$ and
$$S=\frac12\sum_{\rm cyc}\frac{a^3+b^3}{a^2+ab+b^2}.$$

**A pair estimate.** For $a,b>0$,
$$\frac{a^3+b^3}{a^2+ab+b^2}\ge\frac{a+b}3\iff3(a+b)(a^2-ab+b^2)\ge(a+b)(a^2+ab+b^2)\iff2(a-b)^2\ge0.$$

**Conclusion.** Summing the three pair estimates, $S\ge\frac12\cdot\frac{2(a+b+c)}3=\frac{a+b+c}3$, with equality iff $a=b=c$.

**Lesson.** When a cyclic sum has numerators and denominators that differ by a nice factor, add the "mirror" sum: the difference telescopes and the symmetrised sum is easier to bound.
@@@ ineq2-muirhead-through-weighted-am-gm.md
---
title: Muirhead's idea with weighted AM-GM
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: Muirhead, majorization, weighted AM-GM
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$a^5+b^5+c^5\ \ge\ abc\,(a^2+b^2+c^2).$$

## Hint

Write $a^3bc=(a^5)^{3/5}(b^5)^{1/5}(c^5)^{1/5}$ and use weighted AM-GM.

## Solution

**Idea.** The exponent vector $(5,0,0)$ majorises $(3,1,1)$: each term $a^3bc$ is a weighted geometric mean of $a^5,b^5,c^5$.

**Weighted AM-GM.** With weights $\frac35,\frac15,\frac15$,
$$a^3bc=(a^5)^{3/5}(b^5)^{1/5}(c^5)^{1/5}\le\frac{3a^5+b^5+c^5}5.$$
The same holds for $b^3ca$ and $c^3ab$.

**Summation.**
$$abc(a^2+b^2+c^2)=a^3bc+b^3ca+c^3ab\le\frac{5(a^5+b^5+c^5)}5=a^5+b^5+c^5.$$
Equality iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** Muirhead's theorem says that $\sum_{\rm sym}a^{\alpha_1}b^{\alpha_2}c^{\alpha_3}\ge\sum_{\rm sym}a^{\beta_1}b^{\beta_2}c^{\beta_3}$ whenever $\alpha$ majorises $\beta$. Each instance can be proved exactly as above.

**Lesson.** An inequality between symmetric sums of monomials of the same degree is an application of weighted AM-GM: find the weights that turn the smaller monomial into a geometric mean of the larger ones.
@@@ ineq2-a-cyclic-inequality-and-the-middle-variable.md
---
title: A cyclic inequality: a²b + b²c + c²a + abc ≤ 4
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: cyclic inequality, WLOG ordering, AM-GM
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$ with $a+b+c=3$. Prove that
$$a^2b+b^2c+c^2a+abc\le4.$$
When does equality hold?

## Hint

The expression is only cyclic, so you cannot assume $a\ge b\ge c$, but you can assume that $b$ is the middle value. Compare with $b(a+c)^2$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Choose the variable in the middle and merge the other two.

**Reduction.** The expression is invariant under the cyclic shift $(a,b,c)\to(b,c,a)$, and one of the three numbers is between the other two, so we may assume $b$ is between $a$ and $c$. Then $(a-b)(b-c)\ge0$.

**Key identity.** Expanding,
$$b(a+c)^2-\left(a^2b+b^2c+c^2a+abc\right)=abc+bc^2-b^2c-c^2a=c(a-b)(b-c)\ \ge0.$$
(Check: $(a-b)(b-c)=ab-ac-b^2+bc$, and multiplying by $c$ gives $abc-ac^2-b^2c+bc^2$. Wait — this gives $-ac^2$ not $-c^2a$, which is the same term. ✓)

**AM-GM.** Hence the expression is at most $b(a+c)^2=4\cdot b\cdot\frac{a+c}2\cdot\frac{a+c}2\le4\left(\frac{b+(a+c)}3\right)^3=4$, since $b+(a+c)=3$.

**Equality.** We need $c(a-b)(b-c)=0$ and $b=\frac{a+c}2=1$. This gives $a=b=c=1$ or $c=0$, $b=1$, $a=2$ (and the cyclic shifts of the latter).

**Lesson.** For cyclic inequalities, "middle variable" arguments replace the symmetry you do not have: the sign of $(a-b)(b-c)$ gives the one extra inequality you need.
@@@ ineq2-imo-1984-the-pqr-method.md
---
title: IMO 1984: yz + zx + xy − 2xyz
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: IMO 1984, pqr method, case analysis, AM-GM
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $x,y,z\ge0$ with $x+y+z=1$. Prove that
$$0\le xy+yz+zx-2xyz\le\frac7{27}.$$

## Hint

Expand $(1-2x)(1-2y)(1-2z)$ and compare with $xy+yz+zx-2xyz$.

## Solution

**Notation.** Let $q=xy+yz+zx$ and $r=xyz$.

**Lower bound.** Since $x,y,z\le1$,
$$q-2r=\bigl[xy(1-z)+yz(1-x)+zx(1-y)\bigr]+r\ge0,$$
because $xy(1-z)+yz(1-x)+zx(1-y)=q-3r$.

**Upper bound.** Expanding, using $x+y+z=1$,
$$(1-2x)(1-2y)(1-2z)=1-2+4q-8r=-1+4q-8r,$$
so $q-2r=\dfrac{1+P}4$ with $P=(1-2x)(1-2y)(1-2z)$. We must show $P\le\frac1{27}$.
- Two of the factors cannot be negative: $1-2x<0$ and $1-2y<0$ would give $x+y>1$, so $z<0$.
- If exactly one factor is negative, the others are non-negative and $P\le0$.
- If none is negative, their sum is $3-2=1$ and AM-GM gives $P\le\left(\frac13\right)^3=\frac1{27}$.

In all cases $P\le\frac1{27}$, hence $q-2r\le\frac{28}{27\cdot4}=\frac7{27}$.

**Equality.** Upper bound: $x=y=z=\frac13$. Lower bound: e.g. $(1,0,0)$.

**Lesson.** Rewriting a symmetric expression through $p=x+y+z$, $q$, $r$ (the pqr method) can turn it into a product whose sign analysis and AM-GM finish the job.
@@@ ineq2-ravi-substitution-and-the-triangle-product.md
---
title: Ravi substitution: abc ≥ (a + b − c)(b + c − a)(c + a − b)
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: Ravi substitution, triangle inequality, AM-GM
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c$ be the sides of a triangle. Prove that
1. $abc\ge(a+b-c)(b+c-a)(c+a-b)$,
2. $\dfrac a{b+c-a}+\dfrac b{c+a-b}+\dfrac c{a+b-c}\ge3$.

## Hint

Set $x=\frac{b+c-a}2$, $y=\frac{c+a-b}2$, $z=\frac{a+b-c}2$. What do you know about $x,y,z$?

## Solution

**Ravi substitution.** The triangle inequalities say exactly that $x,y,z>0$, and conversely $a=y+z$, $b=z+x$, $c=x+y$. So the sides of a triangle correspond to arbitrary positive numbers.

**1.** In terms of $x,y,z$ the inequality reads
$$(x+y)(y+z)(z+x)\ge(2z)(2x)(2y)=8xyz,$$
which is true by AM-GM on each factor: $x+y\ge2\sqrt{xy}$, etc. Equality iff $x=y=z$, i.e. the triangle is equilateral.

**2.** Since $b+c-a=2x$, etc.,
$$\sum\frac a{b+c-a}=\frac{y+z}{2x}+\frac{z+x}{2y}+\frac{x+y}{2z}=\frac12\left[\left(\frac xy+\frac yx\right)+\left(\frac yz+\frac zy\right)+\left(\frac zx+\frac xz\right)\right]\ge\frac12\cdot6=3.$$

**Lesson.** A triangle condition is a hidden positivity condition: the Ravi substitution replaces it by three free positive variables, which makes many geometric inequalities routine.
@@@ ineq2-minkowski-and-a-sum-of-square-roots.md
---
title: Minkowski's inequality and a sum of two square roots
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: Minkowski, triangle inequality, optimisation
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

1. Prove that for real numbers $p_1,q_1,p_2,q_2$,
$$\sqrt{p_1^2+q_1^2}+\sqrt{p_2^2+q_2^2}\ \ge\ \sqrt{(p_1+p_2)^2+(q_1+q_2)^2}.$$
2. Find the minimum over $x\in\mathbb{R}$ of $\sqrt{x^2+4}+\sqrt{(6-x)^2+9}$ and the $x$ where it is reached.

## Hint

For part 1, interpret $(p_i,q_i)$ as vectors and expand the square of the right-hand side. For part 2, apply part 1 with well-chosen $p_i,q_i$.

## Solution

**1.** Let $u=(p_1,q_1)$ and $v=(p_2,q_2)$. By Cauchy–Schwarz, $u\cdot v\le|u||v|$, so
$$|u+v|^2=|u|^2+2u\cdot v+|v|^2\le|u|^2+2|u||v|+|v|^2=(|u|+|v|)^2.$$
Taking square roots gives the inequality. Equality iff $u,v$ are parallel with the same direction.

**2.** Write the expression as $\sqrt{x^2+2^2}+\sqrt{(6-x)^2+3^2}$ and apply part 1 with $(p_1,q_1)=(x,2)$ and $(p_2,q_2)=(6-x,3)$:
$$\ge\sqrt{6^2+5^2}=\sqrt{61}.$$

**Equality.** The vectors $(x,2)$ and $(6-x,3)$ must be parallel with the same direction: $3x=2(6-x)$, i.e. $x=\frac{12}5>0$. So the minimum is $\sqrt{61}$, reached at $x=\frac{12}5$.

**Remark.** Geometrically, the expression is the length of the broken line from $(0,2)$ to $(x,0)$ to $(6,-3)$; it is shortest when the line is straight, of length $\sqrt{6^2+5^2}$.

**Lesson.** A sum of square roots of sums of two squares is a sum of lengths: think "triangle inequality", and choose the vectors so that the unknown cancels in the sum.
@@@ ineq2-a-chain-of-squares.md
---
title: A chain of squares: a⁴ + b⁴ + c⁴ ≥ abc(a + b + c)
category: algebra/inequalities
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: sum of squares, SOS, real numbers
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Prove that for all real numbers $a,b,c$,
$$a^4+b^4+c^4\ \ge\ abc\,(a+b+c).$$

## Hint

Show the chain $a^4+b^4+c^4\ge a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2\ge abc(a+b+c)$, each step being a sum of squares.

## Solution

**Idea.** Prove a stronger intermediate inequality twice with $x^2+y^2+z^2\ge xy+yz+zx$.

**Basic fact.** For all real $x,y,z$,
$$x^2+y^2+z^2-xy-yz-zx=\frac12\left[(x-y)^2+(y-z)^2+(z-x)^2\right]\ge0.$$

**Step 1.** With $x=a^2$, $y=b^2$, $z=c^2$:
$$a^4+b^4+c^4\ge a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2.$$

**Step 2.** With $x=ab$, $y=bc$, $z=ca$: $xy=ab^2c$, $yz=bc^2a$, $zx=ca^2b$, so
$$a^2b^2+b^2c^2+c^2a^2\ge ab^2c+abc^2+a^2bc=abc(a+b+c).$$

**Equality.** Step 1 is an equality iff $a^2=b^2=c^2$, and step 2 iff $ab=bc=ca$. Together, $|a|=|b|=|c|$ and the signs agree, so $a=b=c$.

**Lesson.** Look for a chain of simpler true inequalities, each of which is "square $\ge0$" after a substitution such as $x=ab$.
@@@ ineq2-jensen-and-the-product-of-sines.md
---
title: The product of the sines of a triangle
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: Jensen, concavity, logarithm, triangle
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $A,B,C$ be the angles of a triangle. Prove that
$$\sin A\sin B\sin C\le\frac{3\sqrt3}8.$$

## Hint

Take logarithms and show that $\ln\sin$ is concave on $(0,\pi)$.

## Solution

**Concavity.** On $(0,\pi)$ the function $g=\ln\sin$ has $g'=\cot$ and $g''=-\dfrac1{\sin^2}<0$, so $g$ is strictly concave.

**Jensen.** For the three angles with equal weights,
$$\frac{g(A)+g(B)+g(C)}3\le g\!\left(\frac{A+B+C}3\right)=g\!\left(\frac\pi3\right)=\ln\frac{\sqrt3}2.$$
Hence $\ln(\sin A\sin B\sin C)\le3\ln\frac{\sqrt3}2$, i.e.
$$\sin A\sin B\sin C\le\left(\frac{\sqrt3}2\right)^3=\frac{3\sqrt3}8.$$

**Equality.** By strict concavity, iff $A=B=C=\frac\pi3$.

**Remark.** The same method gives $\sin\frac A2\sin\frac B2\sin\frac C2\le\frac18$.

**Lesson.** A product under a fixed-sum constraint is a sum after taking logarithms: check the concavity of $\ln f$ and use Jensen.
@@@ ineq2-induction-on-the-number-of-variables.md
---
title: Two inequalities by induction on the number of variables
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: induction, AM-GM, Bernoulli, smoothing
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

1. If $x_1,\dots,x_n>0$ and $x_1x_2\cdots x_n=1$, then $x_1+\dots+x_n\ge n$.
2. If $x_1,\dots,x_n>-1$ all have the same sign, then $(1+x_1)\cdots(1+x_n)\ge1+x_1+\dots+x_n$.

Deduce AM-GM from part 1.

## Hint

For part 1, among $n+1$ numbers with product $1$, one is $\ge1$ and one is $\le1$: merge them. For part 2, multiply the inductive inequality by $1+x_{n+1}>0$.

## Solution

**1.** For $n=1$, $x_1=1$. Assume it holds for $n$ numbers and take $x_1,\dots,x_{n+1}$ with product $1$. Not all are $<1$ and not all $>1$; reorder so that $x_n\ge1\ge x_{n+1}$. The $n$ numbers $x_1,\dots,x_{n-1},x_nx_{n+1}$ have product $1$, so by induction
$$x_1+\dots+x_{n-1}+x_nx_{n+1}\ge n.$$
Therefore
$$\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}x_i\ \ge\ n+x_n+x_{n+1}-x_nx_{n+1}=n+1+(x_n-1)(1-x_{n+1})\ \ge\ n+1.$$

**2.** For $n=1$ it is an equality. Suppose it holds for $n$, and let $S_n=x_1+\dots+x_n$. Since $1+x_{n+1}>0$,
$$\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}(1+x_i)\ge(1+x_{n+1})(1+S_n)=1+S_{n+1}+x_{n+1}S_n\ge1+S_{n+1},$$
because $x_{n+1}$ and $S_n$ have the same sign, so $x_{n+1}S_n\ge0$.

**AM-GM.** For positive $a_1,\dots,a_n$ with geometric mean $g$, set $x_i=a_i/g$: the product of the $x_i$ is $1$, so $\sum a_i/g\ge n$, i.e. $\frac{a_1+\dots+a_n}n\ge g$.

**Lesson.** Induction on the number of variables works when two variables can be merged into one while keeping the constraint: choose the pair on opposite sides of $1$.
@@@ ineq2-abc-equals-one-and-three-fractions.md
---
title: With abc = 1: Σ 1/(a² + a + 1) ≥ 1
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: constraint abc=1, clearing denominators, expansion
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$ with $abc=1$. Prove that
$$\frac1{a^2+a+1}+\frac1{b^2+b+1}+\frac1{c^2+c+1}\ge1.$$

## Hint

Clear the denominators and use $abc=1$ to simplify the many terms containing $abc$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The constraint kills most terms after expansion.

**Clearing denominators.** Let $D=\prod(a^2+a+1)$. The inequality is
$$\sum_{\rm cyc}(b^2+b+1)(c^2+c+1)-D\ge0.$$

**Expansion.** Expanding mechanically gives a polynomial whose terms, after substituting $abc=1$, simplify:
- $-a^2b^2c^2=-1$,
- $-a^2b^2c-a^2bc^2-ab^2c^2=-ab-ac-bc$,
- $-a^2bc-ab^2c-abc^2=-a-b-c$,
- $-abc=-1$.

Combining with the positive terms $a^2+b^2+c^2+a+b+c+2$:
$$a^2+b^2+c^2-ab-bc-ca\ \ge0.$$

**Equality.** Iff $a=b=c=1$.

**Lesson.** With a multiplicative constraint, expand fully and use $abc=1$ to cancel: often the inequality collapses to a classical one such as $a^2+b^2+c^2\ge ab+bc+ca$.
@@@ ineq2-the-cube-substitution.md
---
title: The cube substitution: Σ 1/(a + b + 1) ≤ 1
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: substitution, abc=1, cubes, AM-GM
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$ with $abc=1$. Prove that
$$\frac1{a+b+1}+\frac1{b+c+1}+\frac1{c+a+1}\le1.$$

## Hint

Write $a=x^3$, $b=y^3$, $c=z^3$ with $xyz=1$, and use $x^3+y^3\ge xy(x+y)$.

## Solution

**Substitution.** Let $x=a^{1/3}$, $y=b^{1/3}$, $z=c^{1/3}$, so $xyz=1$.

**Key lemma.** For $x,y>0$, $x^3+y^3-xy(x+y)=(x+y)(x-y)^2\ge0$.

**Estimate.** Using $1=xyz$,
$$a+b+1=x^3+y^3+xyz\ge xy(x+y)+xyz=xy(x+y+z).$$
Since $xy=\frac1z$,
$$\frac1{a+b+1}\le\frac1{xy(x+y+z)}=\frac z{x+y+z}.$$

**Summation.** The same holds for the other two terms, and adding gives
$$\sum\frac1{a+b+1}\le\frac{z+x+y}{x+y+z}=1.$$

**Equality.** Iff $x=y=z=1$.

**Lesson.** The constraint $abc=1$ with cubes in the lemma $x^3+y^3\ge xy(x+y)$ suggests the substitution $a=x^3$: the "1" becomes $xyz$ and the denominators become homogeneous.
@@@ ineq-products-of-sums-and-am-gm.md
---
title: Two inequalities from AM-GM on pairs
category: algebra/inequalities
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: AM-GM, symmetric inequalities, warm-up
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
1. $(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)\ge8abc$,
2. $(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)\ge\dfrac89(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)$.

## Hint

For 1, apply $x+y\ge2\sqrt{xy}$ to each factor. For 2, first expand $(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)$ in terms of $(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)$ and $abc$.

## Solution

**1.** By AM-GM, $a+b\ge2\sqrt{ab}$, $b+c\ge2\sqrt{bc}$, $c+a\ge2\sqrt{ca}$. All sides are positive, so we may multiply:
$$(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)\ge8\sqrt{a^2b^2c^2}=8abc.$$
Equality iff $a=b=c$.

**2.** Expanding, $(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)=\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b+3abc$ and $(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)=\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b+2abc$, where $\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b$ has the six terms $a^2b,a^2c,b^2a,b^2c,c^2a,c^2b$. Hence
$$(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)=(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)-abc.$$
Put $P=(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)$. The claim becomes $P-abc\ge\frac89P$, i.e. $P\ge9abc$. By AM-GM, $a+b+c\ge3\sqrt[3]{abc}$ and $ab+bc+ca\ge3\sqrt[3]{(abc)^2}$; multiplying gives $P\ge9abc$.

**Remark.** Part 1 is a very frequent step: it says that a product of pairwise sums is at least $8$ times the product.

**Lesson.** Apply AM-GM to *small groups* of variables and multiply; and rewrite symmetric expressions with $p=a+b+c$, $q=ab+bc+ca$, $r=abc$.
@@@ ineq-nesbitt-by-cauchy-schwarz.md
---
title: Nesbitt's inequality
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 2
tags: Nesbitt, Cauchy-Schwarz, Titu's lemma
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$\frac a{b+c}+\frac b{c+a}+\frac c{a+b}\ge\frac32.$$

## Hint

Write $\frac a{b+c}=\frac{a^2}{a(b+c)}$ and use Titu's lemma (Cauchy-Schwarz in Engel form): $\sum\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\ge\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum y_i}$ for $y_i>0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Put every fraction in the form $\frac{x^2}{y}$.

**Titu.**
$$\sum\frac a{b+c}=\sum\frac{a^2}{a(b+c)}\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{\sum a(b+c)}=\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(ab+bc+ca)}.$$

**Last step.** $(a+b+c)^2=a^2+b^2+c^2+2(ab+bc+ca)\ge3(ab+bc+ca)$ because $a^2+b^2+c^2\ge ab+bc+ca$ (it is $\frac12\sum(a-b)^2\ge0$). Therefore
$$\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{2(ab+bc+ca)}\ge\frac{3(ab+bc+ca)}{2(ab+bc+ca)}=\frac32.$$

**Equality** iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark (Titu from Cauchy-Schwarz).** $\left(\sum\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\right)\left(\sum y_i\right)\ge\left(\sum x_i\right)^2$ is Cauchy-Schwarz applied to the vectors $\left(\frac{x_i}{\sqrt{y_i}}\right)$ and $\left(\sqrt{y_i}\right)$.

**Lesson.** A sum of fractions $\frac{\text{something}}{\text{something}}$: multiply numerator and denominator to create squares in the numerators, then use Titu.
@@@ ineq-squares-over-a-cyclic-denominator.md
---
title: a²/b + b²/c + c²/a ≥ a + b + c
category: algebra/inequalities
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: Titu's lemma, Cauchy-Schwarz, cyclic sums
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$\frac{a^2}b+\frac{b^2}c+\frac{c^2}a\ge a+b+c.$$

## Hint

Titu's lemma: $\sum\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\ge\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum y_i}$. Choose $x_i$ and $y_i$ directly from the three fractions.

## Solution

**Titu with $(x_i)=(a,b,c)$ and $(y_i)=(b,c,a)$.**
$$\frac{a^2}b+\frac{b^2}c+\frac{c^2}a\ge\frac{(a+b+c)^2}{b+c+a}=a+b+c.$$

**Second proof (tangent trick).** For positive $x,y$: $\frac{x^2}y\ge2x-y$, because $\frac{x^2}y-2x+y=\frac{(x-y)^2}y\ge0$. Hence
$$\sum\frac{a^2}b\ge(2a-b)+(2b-c)+(2c-a)=a+b+c.$$

**Equality** iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** The second proof shows the strength of the inequality $\frac{x^2}y\ge2x-y$: it is the tangent line at $y=x$ of the convex function $y\mapsto\frac{x^2}y$.

**Lesson.** Know the two faces of the same tool: Titu (global) and $\frac{x^2}y\ge2x-y$ (term by term).
@@@ ineq-the-rearrangement-inequality.md
---
title: a³ + b³ + c³ ≥ a²b + b²c + c²a
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 2
tags: rearrangement inequality, AM-GM, cyclic sums
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$. Prove that $a^3+b^3+c^3\ge a^2b+b^2c+c^2a$, in two different ways.

## Hint

(i) The rearrangement inequality: if $x_1\le x_2\le x_3$ and $y_1\le y_2\le y_3$, then $\sum x_iy_i\ge\sum x_iy_{\sigma(i)}$ for every permutation $\sigma$. (ii) Weighted AM-GM on $a^3,a^3,b^3$.

## Solution

**Rearrangement.** The sequences $(a,b,c)$ and $(a^2,b^2,c^2)$ are ordered in the same way. The sum of products of the identically ordered terms is at least the sum for any other pairing:
$$a\cdot a^2+b\cdot b^2+c\cdot c^2\ge b\cdot a^2+c\cdot b^2+a\cdot c^2=a^2b+b^2c+c^2a.$$

**AM-GM.** $\frac{a^3+a^3+b^3}3\ge\sqrt[3]{a^6b^3}=a^2b$, i.e. $2a^3+b^3\ge3a^2b$. Similarly $2b^3+c^3\ge3b^2c$ and $2c^3+a^3\ge3c^2a$. Adding: $3(a^3+b^3+c^3)\ge3(a^2b+b^2c+c^2a)$.

**Equality** iff $a=b=c$.

**Lesson.** Rearrangement is the tool when two sequences are *similarly ordered*; AM-GM with repeated terms is the tool when you need to produce a specific monomial like $a^2b$.
@@@ ineq-chebyshev-and-powers-of-a-b-c.md
---
title: Chebyshev's sum inequality and a^a b^b c^c
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: Chebyshev's sum inequality, logarithm, monotone sequences
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

1. Prove Chebyshev's inequality: if $x_1\le x_2\le x_3$ and $y_1\le y_2\le y_3$ then $3\sum x_iy_i\ge\left(\sum x_i\right)\left(\sum y_i\right)$.
2. Deduce that for $a,b,c>0$: $a^ab^bc^c\ge(abc)^{(a+b+c)/3}$.

## Hint

For 1, expand $\sum_{i,j}(x_i-x_j)(y_i-y_j)$. For 2, apply part 1 to $(a,b,c)$ and $(\ln a,\ln b,\ln c)$.

## Solution

**1.** For all $i,j$, the numbers $x_i-x_j$ and $y_i-y_j$ have the same sign, so $(x_i-x_j)(y_i-y_j)\ge0$. Summing over all ordered pairs $(i,j)$:
$$0\le\sum_{i,j}(x_i-x_j)(y_i-y_j)=2\cdot3\sum_ix_iy_i-2\left(\sum x_i\right)\left(\sum y_j\right).$$
Dividing by $2$ gives the claim.

**2.** Since $\ln$ is increasing, $(a,b,c)$ and $(\ln a,\ln b,\ln c)$ are similarly ordered. By part 1,
$$3(a\ln a+b\ln b+c\ln c)\ge(a+b+c)(\ln a+\ln b+\ln c).$$
Divide by $3$ and apply $\exp$:
$$a^ab^bc^c\ge(abc)^{(a+b+c)/3}.$$

**Equality** iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** The same proof works for any $n$ and any increasing function in place of $\ln$.

**Lesson.** When a sum looks like an "average of a product versus product of averages", Chebyshev is the tool.
@@@ ineq-the-tangent-line-trick.md
---
title: The tangent line trick
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: tangent line trick, constraint a+b+c=1, one-variable reduction
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$ with $a+b+c=1$. Prove that
$$\frac a{a^2+1}+\frac b{b^2+1}+\frac c{c^2+1}\le\frac9{10}.$$

## Hint

Equality should hold at $a=b=c=\frac13$. Find a line $\ell(a)=\alpha a+\beta$, tangent to $f(a)=\frac a{a^2+1}$ at $a=\frac13$, such that $f(a)\le\ell(a)$ for all $a>0$.

## Solution

**Finding the line.** $f\left(\frac13\right)=\frac3{10}$ and $f'(a)=\frac{1-a^2}{(a^2+1)^2}$, so $f'\left(\frac13\right)=\frac{18}{25}$. The tangent is
$$\ell(a)=\frac3{10}+\frac{18}{25}\left(a-\frac13\right)=\frac{18}{25}a+\frac3{50}.$$

**The one-variable inequality.** For $a>0$ we claim $\frac a{a^2+1}\le\frac{18a}{25}+\frac3{50}$. Multiplying by $50(a^2+1)>0$ it reads $50a\le(36a+3)(a^2+1)$, i.e.
$$36a^3+3a^2-14a+3\ge0.$$
This factors as $(3a-1)^2(4a+3)\ge0$, true for $a>0$.

**Summing.** Add the three inequalities and use $a+b+c=1$:
$$\sum\frac a{a^2+1}\le\frac{18}{25}(a+b+c)+\frac9{50}=\frac9{10}.$$

**Remark.** The tangent line trick works when the constraint is linear and the function is locally below its tangent.

**Lesson.** Constraint $a+b+c=\text{const}$ and a sum $f(a)+f(b)+f(c)$: guess the equality case, compute the tangent at that point, and check the one-variable inequality.
@@@ ineq-jensen-with-a-convex-function.md
---
title: A sum of squares of a + 1/a, via Jensen
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: Jensen's inequality, convexity, constraint a+b+c=1
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$ with $a+b+c=1$. Prove that
$$\left(a+\frac1a\right)^2+\left(b+\frac1b\right)^2+\left(c+\frac1c\right)^2\ge\frac{100}3.$$

## Hint

Show that $f(x)=\left(x+\frac1x\right)^2$ is convex on $(0,\infty)$ and apply Jensen's inequality.

## Solution

**Convexity.** $f(x)=x^2+2+\frac1{x^2}$, so
$$f''(x)=2+\frac6{x^4}>0\quad(x>0).$$

**Jensen.** For a convex $f$: $\frac{f(a)+f(b)+f(c)}3\ge f\left(\frac{a+b+c}3\right)=f\left(\frac13\right)$. Here $f\left(\frac13\right)=\left(\frac13+3\right)^2=\frac{100}9$. Hence
$$f(a)+f(b)+f(c)\ge3\cdot\frac{100}9=\frac{100}3.$$

**Equality** iff $a=b=c=\frac13$.

**Remark.** An alternative is the tangent line trick at $\frac13$.

**Lesson.** If $\sum f(a_i)$ has a *fixed sum* $\sum a_i$ and $f''\ge0$, Jensen gives the minimum at the equal point; $f''\le0$ gives the maximum.
@@@ ineq-imo-1995-with-the-constraint-abc-equals-1.md
---
title: A classic with abc = 1 (IMO 1995)
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: IMO, substitution, Titu's lemma, abc=1
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$ with $abc=1$. Prove that
$$\frac1{a^3(b+c)}+\frac1{b^3(c+a)}+\frac1{c^3(a+b)}\ge\frac32.$$

## Hint

Substitute $x=\frac1a$, $y=\frac1b$, $z=\frac1c$ (so $xyz=1$) and simplify each term.

## Solution

**Substitution.** With $x=\frac1a$ etc., $xyz=1$ and
$$\frac1{a^3(b+c)}=\frac{x^3}{\frac1y+\frac1z}=\frac{x^3yz}{y+z}=\frac{x^2}{y+z}.$$

**Titu.**
$$\sum\frac{x^2}{y+z}\ge\frac{(x+y+z)^2}{2(x+y+z)}=\frac{x+y+z}2.$$

**AM-GM.** $x+y+z\ge3\sqrt[3]{xyz}=3$, so the left side is at least $\frac32$.

**Equality** iff $a=b=c=1$.

**Remark.** The condition $abc=1$ was used twice: to simplify $x^3yz$ and to bound $x+y+z$.

**Lesson.** With $abc=1$, try $a=\frac1x$ (or $a=\frac yx$, $b=\frac zy$, $c=\frac xz$): it turns the condition into something usable and often reveals a known inequality.
@@@ ineq-schur-s-inequality.md
---
title: Schur's inequality and a bound with a + b + c = 1
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Schur's inequality, IMO 1984, symmetric polynomials, ordering variables
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

1. Prove Schur's inequality: for $a,b,c\ge0$,
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-a)(b-c)+c(c-a)(c-b)\ge0.$$
2. Deduce $(a+b+c)^3+9abc\ge4(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)$.
3. For $x,y,z\ge0$ with $x+y+z=1$, prove $0\le xy+yz+zx-2xyz\le\dfrac7{27}$.

## Hint

For 1, assume $a\ge b\ge c$ and group the first two terms. For 3, use $p=1$, $q=xy+yz+zx$, $r=xyz$ and part 2 for the upper bound.

## Solution

**1.** The expression is symmetric, so assume $a\ge b\ge c$. Then
$$a(a-b)(a-c)+b(b-a)(b-c)=(a-b)^2(a+b-c)\ge0,$$
and $c(c-a)(c-b)=c(a-c)(b-c)\ge0$. Hence the sum is $\ge0$. Equality iff $a=b=c$, or $a=b$ and $c=0$.

**2.** Expanding, Schur's inequality reads $a^3+b^3+c^3+3abc\ge\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b$. Meanwhile
$$(a+b+c)^3=\sum a^3+3\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b+6abc,\qquad(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)=\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b+3abc.$$
So
$$(a+b+c)^3+9abc-4(a+b+c)(ab+bc+ca)=\sum a^3-\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b+3abc\ge0.$$

**3. Upper bound.** With $p=1$, part 2 gives $q\le\frac{1+9r}4$. By AM-GM $r\le\frac1{27}$, hence
$$q-2r\le\frac{1+9r}4-2r\le\frac14+\frac r4\le\frac7{27}.$$
Equality at $x=y=z=\frac13$.

**Lower bound.** Let $z\le\frac13$ be the smallest. Then
$$xy+yz+zx-2xyz=xy(1-2z)+z(x+y)\ge0.$$

**Remark.** Part 3 is IMO 1984 problem 1.

**Lesson.** When a symmetric degree-3 inequality has equality at $a=b=c$ *and* at $(1,1,0)$, think Schur.
@@@ ineq-vasc-s-inequality-by-sos.md
---
title: Vasc's inequality by a sum of squares
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: SOS, sum of squares, cyclic inequality, Vasc's inequality
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Prove that for all real numbers $a,b,c$,
$$(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2\ge3\left(a^3b+b^3c+c^3a\right).$$

## Hint

Try to write $2\Bigl[(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2-3(a^3b+b^3c+c^3a)\Bigr]$ as a sum of three squares of quadratic forms obtained from each other by the cyclic shift $a\to b\to c\to a$.

## Solution

**Idea.** For cyclic inequalities, AM-GM and Cauchy-Schwarz often fail because the equality cases are strange. A sum-of-squares certificate proves the inequality for *all real* numbers at once.

**Identity.** Define
$$S_1=a^2-2ab+bc-c^2+ca,\quad S_2=b^2-2bc+ca-a^2+ab,\quad S_3=c^2-2ca+ab-b^2+bc.$$
Direct expansion gives
$$S_1^2+S_2^2+S_3^2=2\Bigl[(a^2+b^2+c^2)^2-3(a^3b+b^3c+c^3a)\Bigr].$$

**Conclusion.** The left side is a sum of squares, hence $\ge0$, so the inequality holds.

**Equality.** Iff $S_1=S_2=S_3=0$. Besides $a=b=c$, there are other equality cases of the form $\left(\sin^2\frac{4\pi}7,\sin^2\frac{2\pi}7,\sin^2\frac\pi7\right)$ up to scaling.

**Remark.** This is Vasile Cîrtoaje's inequality.

**Lesson.** For a polynomial inequality valid on all of $\mathbb{R}^n$, try to write the difference as a sum of squares (SOS).
@@@ ineq-mixing-variables-and-a-bound-of-4-over-27.md
---
title: Mixing variables: a²b + b²c + c²a ≤ 4/27
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: mixing variables, cyclic sums, ordering, AM-GM
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c\ge0$ with $a+b+c=1$. Prove that
$$a^2b+b^2c+c^2a\le\frac4{27},$$
and find all cases of equality.

## Hint

The sum is cyclic, not symmetric: you can rotate the variables but not swap them. Among $a,b,c$, one lies between the other two; rotate so that it is $b$. Show then that $a^2b+b^2c+c^2a+abc\le b(a+c)^2$.

## Solution

**Reduction.** The expression is invariant under the rotation $a\to b\to c\to a$. Among three numbers, one is between the other two; by rotating, we may assume $b$ lies between $a$ and $c$, so $(a-b)(b-c)\ge0$.

**Key identity.** Expanding,
$$b(a+c)^2-\left(a^2b+b^2c+c^2a+abc\right)=c(a-b)(b-c)\ge0.$$

**Conclusion.** Since $abc\ge0$,
$$a^2b+b^2c+c^2a\le b(a+c)^2=4\cdot b\cdot\frac{a+c}2\cdot\frac{a+c}2\le4\left(\frac{b+\frac{a+c}2+\frac{a+c}2}3\right)^3=\frac4{27}.$$

**Equality.** We need $abc=0$, $c(a-b)(b-c)=0$ and $b=\frac{a+c}2$ simultaneously. This gives $(a,b,c)=\left(\frac23,\frac13,0\right)$ and its cyclic rotations.

**Remark.** The maximum is *not* at $a=b=c$ (where the value is $\frac19=\frac3{27}$): it is on the boundary.

**Lesson.** Cyclic inequality with an equality case on the boundary: order the variables (carefully, only using rotations) and push the expression to a two-variable one.
@@@ ineq-imo-2001-by-weighted-jensen.md
---
title: IMO 2001: a/√(a² + 8bc)
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: IMO, weighted Jensen, convexity, normalisation
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$. Prove that
$$\frac a{\sqrt{a^2+8bc}}+\frac b{\sqrt{b^2+8ca}}+\frac c{\sqrt{c^2+8ab}}\ge1.$$

## Hint

Denote $X_a=a^2+8bc$, etc., and $s=a+b+c$. The function $t\mapsto t^{-1/2}$ is convex. Apply Jensen with the weights $\frac as,\frac bs,\frac cs$.

## Solution

**Weighted Jensen.** For a convex $f$ and weights $w_i\ge0$ with $\sum w_i=1$: $\sum w_if(t_i)\ge f\left(\sum w_it_i\right)$. Take $f(t)=t^{-1/2}$, which is convex on $(0,\infty)$ since $f''(t)=\frac34t^{-5/2}>0$, with $w_a=\frac as$ and $t_a=X_a$:
$$\sum\frac as\cdot\frac1{\sqrt{X_a}}\ge\frac1{\sqrt{\sum\frac as X_a}}.$$
Multiplying by $s$,
$$\sum\frac a{\sqrt{X_a}}\ge\frac{s^{3/2}}{\sqrt{\sum aX_a}}.$$

**It suffices to prove** $s^3\ge\sum aX_a=a^3+b^3+c^3+24abc$. Now
$$s^3-\left(\sum a^3+24abc\right)=3\left(\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b-6abc\right)\ge0,$$
because $\sum_{\rm sym}a^2b\ge6abc$.

**Equality** iff $a=b=c$.

**Remark.** The official solutions use Hölder's inequality or the bound $\frac a{\sqrt{a^2+8bc}}\ge\frac{a^{4/3}}{a^{4/3}+b^{4/3}+c^{4/3}}$.

**Lesson.** A sum $\sum\frac a{\sqrt{X_a}}$ with the *numerator also a weight* suggests Jensen with weights proportional to the numerators.
@@@ ineq-weighted-am-gm.md
---
title: Weighted AM-GM: how to choose the splitting
category: algebra/inequalities
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: weighted AM-GM, optimisation, equality case
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

1. Let $x,y>0$ with $x+y=5$. Find the maximum of $x^2y^3$.
2. Prove that for $a,b>0$, $2a+3b\ge5\,a^{2/5}b^{3/5}$.

## Hint

You want AM-GM on $5$ numbers whose sum is the *constraint* and whose product is the target. Split $x$ into $2$ equal parts and $y$ into $3$ equal parts, with the right scaling so that the equality case is reachable.

## Solution

**1.** Apply AM-GM to the five numbers $\frac x2,\frac x2,\frac y3,\frac y3,\frac y3$, whose sum is $x+y=5$:
$$\left(\frac x2\right)^2\left(\frac y3\right)^3\le\left(\frac{x+y}5\right)^5=1.$$
Hence $x^2y^3\le4\cdot27=108$. Equality iff all five numbers are equal: $\frac x2=\frac y3$, i.e. $x=2$, $y=3$. The maximum is $108$.

**2.** AM-GM on the five numbers $a,a,b,b,b$ gives
$$\frac{2a+3b}5\ge\sqrt[5]{a^2b^3}\iff2a+3b\ge5a^{2/5}b^{3/5}.$$
In general, for $\lambda\in(0,1)$: $\lambda a+(1-\lambda)b\ge a^\lambda b^{1-\lambda}$.

**Remark.** Compare with calculus: maximizing $x^2(5-x)^3$ by differentiation gives the same point.

**Lesson.** To maximize a product under a sum constraint, split the variables into equal parts proportional to their exponents.
@@@ ineq-cauchy-schwarz-with-weights.md
---
title: Minimising 1/a + 4/b + 9/c on a + b + c = 1
category: algebra/inequalities
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: Cauchy-Schwarz, constrained minimum, equality case
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c>0$ with $a+b+c=1$. Find the minimum of $\dfrac1a+\dfrac4b+\dfrac9c$ and the point where it is attained.

## Hint

Multiply the sum by $a+b+c=1$ and apply Cauchy-Schwarz to the vectors $\left(\frac1{\sqrt a},\frac2{\sqrt b},\frac3{\sqrt c}\right)$ and $\left(\sqrt a,\sqrt b,\sqrt c\right)$.

## Solution

**Cauchy-Schwarz.**
$$\left(\frac1a+\frac4b+\frac9c\right)(a+b+c)\ge\left(1+2+3\right)^2=36.$$
Since $a+b+c=1$, $\frac1a+\frac4b+\frac9c\ge36$.

**Equality.** In Cauchy-Schwarz the two vectors are proportional: $\frac1{\sqrt a}:\sqrt a=\frac2{\sqrt b}:\sqrt b=\frac3{\sqrt c}:\sqrt c$, i.e. $a:b:c=1:2:3$. With $a+b+c=1$: $(a,b,c)=\left(\frac16,\frac13,\frac12\right)$, and indeed $6+12+18=36$.

**Remark.** This is the Engel form: $\sum\frac{x_i^2}{y_i}\ge\frac{(\sum x_i)^2}{\sum y_i}$ with $x=(1,2,3)$ and $y=(a,b,c)$.

**Lesson.** To find an extremum with Cauchy-Schwarz, *first* guess the equality case ($a:b:c$), which tells you how to choose the vectors.

@@@ alg2-the-derivative-of-a-polynomial-with-only-real-roots.md
---
title: The derivative keeps all roots real
category: algebra/polynomials
level: m1
difficulty: 3
tags: polynomials, Rolle's theorem, multiplicity, real roots
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $P$ be a real polynomial of degree $n\ge1$ all of whose $n$ complex roots (counted with multiplicity) are real. Prove that $P'$ also has all its $n-1$ roots real.

## Hint

Let $r_1<\dots<r_k$ be the distinct roots of $P$, with multiplicities $m_1,\dots,m_k$. First find how many roots of $P'$ sit at the points $r_i$ themselves, then look for roots between consecutive $r_i$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Count the roots of $P'$ that we can *see* and show that there are already $n-1=\deg P'$ of them.

**Roots at the $r_i$.** Write $P(x)=(x-r_i)^{m_i}Q(x)$ with $Q(r_i)\ne0$. Then $P'(x)=(x-r_i)^{m_i-1}\bigl(m_iQ(x)+(x-r_i)Q'(x)\bigr)$ and the second factor equals $m_iQ(r_i)\ne0$ at $r_i$. So $r_i$ is a root of $P'$ of multiplicity exactly $m_i-1$ (no root if $m_i=1$). Together these give $\sum_i(m_i-1)=n-k$ roots of $P'$.

**Roots between the $r_i$.** For $1\le i\le k-1$, $P(r_i)=P(r_{i+1})=0$, so by Rolle's theorem $P'$ vanishes at some point of the open interval $(r_i,r_{i+1})$. These $k-1$ points are distinct from each other and from all the $r_j$.

**Conclusion.** $P'$ has at least $(n-k)+(k-1)=n-1$ real roots counted with multiplicity. Since $\deg P'=n-1$, these are all its roots, and they are all real. $\blacksquare$

**Example.** $P(x)=x^3-3x+2=(x-1)^2(x+2)$: $P'(x)=3(x-1)(x+1)$, with the roots $1$ (from the double root) and $-1\in(-2,1)$ (from Rolle).

**Lesson.** Rolle's theorem counts roots: between two roots of $P$ there is a root of $P'$, and a multiple root of $P$ stays a root of $P'$. Count both and compare with the degree.
@@@ alg2-counting-real-roots-of-x-to-the-fifth-minus-5x-plus-a.md
---
title: How many real roots does x⁵ − 5x + a have?
category: algebra/polynomials
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: polynomials, derivative, number of roots, parameter
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

For a real number $a$, determine the number of distinct real roots of $P_a(x)=x^5-5x+a$.

## Hint

$P_a'(x)=5(x^4-1)$. A polynomial is monotone between consecutive critical points, so it has at most one root in each such interval.

## Solution

**Critical points.** $P_a'(x)=5(x^2-1)(x^2+1)$ vanishes only at $x=\pm1$. Hence $P_a$ is increasing on $(-\infty,-1]$, decreasing on $[-1,1]$ and increasing on $[1,+\infty)$.

**Values.** $P_a(-1)=4+a$ (local maximum) and $P_a(1)=a-4$ (local minimum). Moreover $P_a(x)\to-\infty$ as $x\to-\infty$ and $P_a(x)\to+\infty$ as $x\to+\infty$.

**Counting.** On each of the three monotone pieces there is at most one root, and by the intermediate value theorem there is one exactly when the endpoint values (or limits) have opposite signs or one is zero.
- $|a|<4$: $P_a(-1)>0>P_a(1)$. One root in each piece: **three** roots.
- $a=4$: $P_a(-1)=8>0$ and $P_a(1)=0$. The root $x=1$ is a double root and there is one more root on $(-\infty,-1)$: **two** distinct roots.
- $a=-4$: symmetric: $P_a(-1)=0$ is a double root and there is one root on $(1,\infty)$: **two** distinct roots.
- $a>4$: $P_a(1)>0$, so $P_a>0$ on $[-1,\infty)$. One root, on $(-\infty,-1)$: **one** root.
- $a<-4$: symmetric, **one** root on $(1,\infty)$.

**Lesson.** To count the real roots of a polynomial depending on a parameter, study the critical values: roots appear or disappear exactly when a critical value crosses $0$.
@@@ alg2-polynomials-satisfying-p-of-x-times-p-of-x-minus-one-equals-p-of-x-squared.md
---
title: P(x)P(x − 1) = P(x²)
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: polynomial equation, roots, unit circle, functional equation
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Find all polynomials $P$ with real coefficients such that $P(x)\,P(x-1)=P(x^2)$ for all real $x$.

## Hint

Check that $x^2+x+1$ works, using $(x^2+x+1)(x^2-x+1)=x^4+x^2+1$. For the converse, show that if $a$ is a root then $a^2$ and $(a+1)^2$ are roots too, and use that the set of roots is finite.

## Solution

**Answer.** $P=0$ and $P(x)=(x^2+x+1)^n$ for $n\ge0$.

**These work.** $(x^2+x+1)^n\bigl((x-1)^2+(x-1)+1\bigr)^n=(x^2+x+1)^n(x^2-x+1)^n=(x^4+x^2+1)^n=P(x^2)$.

**Converse.** Let $P\ne0$ be a solution and let $R$ be its (finite) set of complex roots. If $R=\varnothing$, $P$ is a constant $c$ with $c^2=c$, so $c=1$. Assume $R\ne\varnothing$ and let $a\in R$.

*Two closure properties.* Putting $x=a$ gives $P(a^2)=P(a)P(a-1)=0$, so $a^2\in R$. Putting $x=a+1$ gives $P((a+1)^2)=P(a+1)P(a)=0$, so $(a+1)^2\in R$.

*Roots have modulus $0$ or $1$.* By the first property, $a,a^2,a^4,\dots$ all lie in the finite set $R$, so $a^{2^j}=a^{2^k}$ for some $j<k$. Taking moduli, $|a|^{2^j}=|a|^{2^k}$, so $|a|\in\{0,1\}$. The same applies to the root $(a+1)^2$: either $(a+1)^2=0$ or $|a+1|=1$.

*$0$ and $-1$ are not roots.* If $0\in R$, then $(0+1)^2=1\in R$ and then $(1+1)^2=4\in R$, but $|4|\notin\{0,1\}$: contradiction. If $-1\in R$, then $(-1+1)^2=0\in R$, which we just excluded.

*All roots are $\omega,\bar\omega$.* So every root has $|a|=1$ and $|a+1|=1$. From $|a+1|^2=|a|^2+2\operatorname{Re}a+1=2+2\operatorname{Re}a=1$ we get $\operatorname{Re}a=-\tfrac12$, so $a=-\tfrac12\pm\tfrac{\sqrt3}2i$, the roots of $x^2+x+1$.
