*Conclusion.* $P(x)=c\,(x-\omega)^p(x-\bar\omega)^q$. Real coefficients force $p=q=n$, so $P=c(x^2+x+1)^n$. Comparing leading coefficients gives $c^2=c$, so $c=1$. $\blacksquare$

**Lesson.** For $P(f(x))$-type equations, study how the equation moves roots around: it produces infinite orbits of roots, which a polynomial cannot have unless the orbit is finite.
@@@ alg2-finite-differences-and-binomial-alternating-sums.md
---
title: Finite differences and alternating binomial sums
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: finite differences, polynomials, binomial coefficients, degree
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $P$ be a polynomial of degree $d$ with leading coefficient $a$ and let $n\ge0$ be an integer. Prove:
$$\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\binom nkP(k)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }d<n,\\(-1)^n\,n!\,a&\text{if }d=n.\end{cases}$$
Deduce that $\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^4(-1)^k\binom4kk^m=0$ for $m=0,1,2,3$ and $=24$ for $m=4$.

## Hint

Define the difference operator $(\Delta P)(x)=P(x+1)-P(x)$. What happens to the degree of $P$ when we apply $\Delta$?

## Solution

**Step 1: $\Delta$ lowers the degree by $1$.** If $P(x)=ax^d+\cdots$ with $d\ge1$, then $(x+1)^d-x^d=dx^{d-1}+\cdots$, so $\Delta P$ has degree $d-1$ and leading coefficient $da$. If $P$ is constant, $\Delta P=0$. By induction, if $\deg P=d$ then $\Delta^dP=a\,d!$ (a constant) and $\Delta^{m}P=0$ for $m>d$.

**Step 2: a formula for $\Delta^n$.** Let $E$ be the shift $(EP)(x)=P(x+1)$ and $I$ the identity. Then $\Delta=E-I$, and $E$, $I$ commute, so the binomial theorem applies:
$$\Delta^n=(E-I)^n=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^{n-k}\binom nkE^k,\qquad\text{so}\qquad(\Delta^nP)(0)=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^{n-k}\binom nkP(k).$$

**Step 3.** Multiplying by $(-1)^n$:
$$\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\binom nkP(k)=(-1)^n(\Delta^nP)(0).$$
By Step 1 this is $0$ if $d<n$ and $(-1)^n\,n!\,a$ if $d=n$. $\blacksquare$

**Check.** For $P(x)=x^m$ with $n=4$: the sums for $m=0,\dots,4$ are $0,0,0,0,24$.

**Lesson.** $\Delta$ is "differentiation for sequences": it lowers the degree by one, and $n$ applications expand into an alternating binomial sum.
@@@ alg2-the-polynomial-taking-the-values-one-over-binomial-coefficients.md
---
title: A polynomial that equals 1 / C(n+1, k) at k = 0, …, n
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: polynomials, interpolation, finite differences, binomial coefficients
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $P$ be a polynomial of degree at most $n$ such that $P(k)=\dfrac1{\binom{n+1}k}$ for $k=0,1,\dots,n$. Find $P(n+1)$.

## Hint

Use the vanishing alternating sum for polynomials of degree less than $N$: with $N=n+1$, $\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}(-1)^k\binom{n+1}kP(k)=0$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The weights $\binom{n+1}k$ in the alternating sum exactly cancel the given values.

**The fact used.** For a polynomial $Q$ of degree $<N$, $\sum_{k=0}^N(-1)^k\binom NkQ(k)=(-1)^N(\Delta^NQ)(0)=0$, because $\Delta$ lowers the degree by one at each application and $\deg P\le n<n+1=N$.

**Apply it with $N=n+1$.**
$$0=\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^k\binom{n+1}k\cdot\frac1{\binom{n+1}k}+(-1)^{n+1}\binom{n+1}{n+1}P(n+1)=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k+(-1)^{n+1}P(n+1).$$

**Evaluate.** $\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k$ equals $1$ if $n$ is even and $0$ if $n$ is odd. Hence
$$P(n+1)=(-1)^n\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k=\begin{cases}1&n\text{ even},\\0&n\text{ odd}.\end{cases}$$

**Check.** $n=1$: $P(0)=1$, $P(1)=\frac12$ gives $P(x)=1-\frac x2$ and $P(2)=0$ ✓. $n=2$: $P(0)=1$, $P(1)=\frac13$, $P(2)=\frac13$ gives $P(3)=1$ ✓.

**Lesson.** When a polynomial of degree $\le n$ is known at $n+1$ points, finite differences give its value at the next point with no interpolation formula.
@@@ alg2-a-polynomial-cannot-take-only-prime-values.md
---
title: No polynomial takes only prime values
category: algebra/polynomials
level: m1
difficulty: 3
tags: integer polynomials, primes, divisibility, congruences
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $P$ be a non-constant polynomial with integer coefficients. Prove that there are infinitely many integers $n$ for which $|P(n)|$ is not a prime number.

## Hint

Take $n_0$ with $m=P(n_0)$ and $|m|\ge2$. For which $k$ is $P(n_0+km)$ divisible by $m$? Why can $P(n_0+km)$ not equal $\pm m$ for most $k$?

## Solution

**A value of absolute value at least $2$.** A non-constant polynomial is unbounded, so there is an integer $n_0$ with $|P(n_0)|\ge2$. Put $m=P(n_0)$.

**Divisibility.** For integer polynomials, $a-b\mid P(a)-P(b)$. With $a=n_0+km$, $b=n_0$ we get $m\mid P(n_0+km)-m$, so $m\mid P(n_0+km)$ for every integer $k$.

**Large values.** A non-constant polynomial takes each value at most $\deg P$ times, so for all but finitely many $k$, $P(n_0+km)\notin\{m,-m\}$. Taking also $|n_0+km|$ large enough that $|P(n_0+km)|>|m|$, we get a multiple of $m$ whose absolute value is strictly larger than $|m|\ge2$.

**Conclusion.** Such a number $|P(n_0+km)|$ has the divisor $|m|$ with $1<|m|<|P(n_0+km)|$, so it is composite. This happens for infinitely many $k$, giving infinitely many integers $n=n_0+km$. $\blacksquare$

**Remark.** Famous examples such as $n^2+n+41$ are prime for many consecutive $n$ (here $n=0,\dots,39$), but must fail eventually (at $n=41$, $P(41)=41\cdot43$).

**Lesson.** The congruence $a-b\mid P(a)-P(b)$ lets you make a polynomial value divisible by a number you control.
@@@ alg2-the-polynomial-with-p-of-x-squared-plus-one.md
---
title: P(x² + 1) = P(x)² + 1
category: algebra/polynomials
level: m1
difficulty: 3
tags: polynomial equation, recursive sequences, identity theorem
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Find all polynomials $P$ with real coefficients such that $P(0)=0$ and $P(x^2+1)=P(x)^2+1$ for all real $x$.

## Hint

Define $a_0=0$ and $a_{k+1}=a_k^2+1$. What can you say about $P(a_k)$?

## Solution

**Answer.** $P(x)=x$.

**It works.** $P(x)=x$ gives $x^2+1=x^2+1$ and $P(0)=0$.

**Uniqueness.** Let $a_0=0$, $a_1=1$, $a_2=2$, $a_3=5,\dots$ with $a_{k+1}=a_k^2+1$. We prove by induction that $P(a_k)=a_k$. For $k=0$ this is $P(0)=0$. If $P(a_k)=a_k$, then the equation with $x=a_k$ gives
$$P(a_{k+1})=P(a_k^2+1)=P(a_k)^2+1=a_k^2+1=a_{k+1}.$$

**Infinitely many roots.** The sequence $(a_k)$ is strictly increasing ($a_{k+1}-a_k=a_k^2-a_k+1>0$), so the $a_k$ are pairwise distinct. The polynomial $P(x)-x$ has infinitely many roots, so it is the zero polynomial: $P(x)=x$. $\blacksquare$

**Lesson.** A functional equation for polynomials plus one starting value often generates infinitely many points where the polynomial agrees with a known one.
@@@ alg2-polynomials-with-p-of-x-squared-equal-to-p-of-x-squared-form.md
---
title: P(x²) = P(x)²
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: polynomial equation, coefficients, degree, comparing terms
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Find all real polynomials $P$ such that $P(x^2)=P(x)^2$ for all real $x$.

## Hint

Compare the two highest-degree terms of $P$. Where does the second-highest term of $P(x)^2$ land, and can $P(x^2)$ have a term there?

## Solution

**Answer.** $P=0$ and $P(x)=x^n$ for $n\ge0$ (including $P=1$).

**These work.** $P(x^2)=x^{2n}=(x^n)^2$.

**Converse.** Let $P\ne0$ have degree $n$ and leading coefficient $a$. Comparing the coefficients of $x^{2n}$: $a=a^2$, so $a=1$.

Suppose $P$ has another nonzero term, and let $bx^j$ ($b\ne0$, $j<n$) be the highest of the remaining terms. Look at the coefficient of $x^{n+j}$.

*In $P(x)^2$:* it is a sum of $c_ic_{i'}$ over pairs of exponents $(i,i')$ with $i+i'=n+j$ and both coefficients nonzero. If neither exponent is $n$, both are $\le j$, and $i+i'\le2j<n+j$: impossible. So one exponent is $n$ and the other is $j$, and the coefficient is $2\cdot1\cdot b=2b\ne0$.

*In $P(x^2)$:* only even exponents $2e$ appear, where $e$ is an exponent of $P$. For $x^{n+j}$ we would need $e=\frac{n+j}2$, which lies strictly between $j$ and $n$, and $P$ has no nonzero term there. So the coefficient is $0$.

The two sides differ: contradiction. Hence $P$ has a single nonzero term, which is $x^n$. $\blacksquare$

**Lesson.** When comparing coefficients in a polynomial identity, look at the leading term and the *next* term: one of the two sides often cannot produce the exponent the other one does.
@@@ alg2-the-sum-of-the-reciprocals-of-x-minus-the-roots-of-unity.md
---
title: Sums over roots of unity from a logarithmic derivative
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: roots of unity, logarithmic derivative, complex numbers, symmetry
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge2$ and $\omega=e^{2\pi i/n}$.

1. Prove that for every complex $x$ that is not an $n$-th root of unity,
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac1{x-\omega^k}=\frac{nx^{n-1}}{x^n-1}.$$
2. Compute $\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac1{2-\omega^k}$.
3. Prove that $\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac1{1-\omega^k}=\frac{n-1}2$.

## Hint

For part 1, $x^n-1=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}(x-\omega^k)$. Differentiate the product and divide by the product. For part 3, pair $k$ with $n-k$.

## Solution

**1.** Let $f(x)=x^n-1=\prod_k(x-\omega^k)$. By the product rule,
$$\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac1{x-\omega^k}.$$
Since $f'(x)=nx^{n-1}$, the identity follows.

**2.** Take $x=2$:
$$\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac1{2-\omega^k}=\frac{n\,2^{n-1}}{2^n-1}.$$
For $n=3$ this is $\frac{12}7\approx1.714$.

**3.** The terms $k$ and $n-k$ have $\omega^{n-k}=\omega^{-k}=\overline{\omega^k}$. For $z=\omega^k\ne1$ with $|z|=1$,
$$\frac1{1-z}+\frac1{1-\frac1z}=\frac1{1-z}+\frac z{z-1}=\frac{1-z}{1-z}=1.$$
So the terms $k=1,\dots,n-1$ can be grouped into pairs $\{k,n-k\}$ each summing to $1$. If $n$ is even, the term $k=\frac n2$ has $\omega^k=-1$ and equals $\frac12$, which is half of a "pair with itself". In all cases the total is $\frac{n-1}2$. $\blacksquare$

**Lesson.** A logarithmic derivative turns a product over roots into a sum over roots; pairing $z$ with $\bar z=1/z$ evaluates sums over the unit circle.
@@@ alg2-every-modulus-divides-a-fibonacci-number.md
---
title: Every integer divides some Fibonacci number
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: Fibonacci numbers, periodicity, pigeonhole, modular arithmetic
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $F_0=0$, $F_1=1$, $F_{n+1}=F_n+F_{n-1}$. Prove that for every integer $m\ge2$ there is a positive integer $p$ such that $m\mid F_p$.

## Hint

Look at the pairs $(F_n,F_{n+1})\bmod m$. There are only $m^2$ possible pairs. Show that the map producing the next pair can be undone.

## Solution

**Idea.** The sequence of pairs is eventually periodic, and because the rule is reversible it is *purely* periodic: it returns to its starting pair $(0,1)$.

**The pair map.** Let $T(a,b)=(b,a+b)$ on $(\mathbb{Z}/m)^2$. Then $(F_n,F_{n+1})\bmod m=T^n(0,1)$. The map $T$ is injective: if $T(a,b)=T(a',b')$ then $b=b'$ and $a+b=a'+b'$, so $a=a'$.

**Pigeonhole.** The $m^2+1$ pairs $T^0(0,1),\dots,T^{m^2}(0,1)$ cannot all be different, so $T^i(0,1)=T^j(0,1)$ for some $0\le i<j$. Since $T$ is injective, we can cancel one $T$ at a time: $(0,1)=T^{j-i}(0,1)$.

**Conclusion.** With $p=j-i>0$ we have $(F_p,F_{p+1})\equiv(0,1)\pmod m$. In particular $m\mid F_p$. $\blacksquare$

**Remark.** The least such $p$ is the "rank of apparition" of $m$: e.g. $5\mid F_5=5$ and $9\mid F_{12}=144$.

**Lesson.** A recurrence determined by a bijective step, taken modulo $m$, is purely periodic.
@@@ alg2-the-gcd-of-two-fibonacci-numbers.md
---
title: gcd(F_m, F_n) = F_gcd(m,n)
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Fibonacci numbers, gcd, Euclidean algorithm, identities
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

With $F_0=0$, $F_1=1$, $F_{n+1}=F_n+F_{n-1}$, prove that for all positive integers $m,n$,
$$\gcd(F_m,F_n)=F_{\gcd(m,n)}.$$
Deduce that if $F_n$ is prime and $n>4$, then $n$ is prime.

## Hint

First prove $F_{a+b}=F_{a+1}F_b+F_aF_{b-1}$ and $\gcd(F_n,F_{n+1})=1$. Then show $\gcd(F_{a+b},F_b)=\gcd(F_a,F_b)$ and imitate the Euclidean algorithm on the indices.

## Solution

**Step 1: addition formula.** For $b\ge1$: $F_{a+b}=F_{a+1}F_b+F_aF_{b-1}$. Proven by induction on $b$ (both sides satisfy the Fibonacci recurrence in $b$).

**Step 2: consecutive terms are coprime.** $\gcd(F_{n+1},F_n)=\gcd(F_n+F_{n-1},F_n)=\gcd(F_{n-1},F_n)=\dots=\gcd(F_1,F_0)=1$.

**Step 3: the key reduction.** By Step 1, $F_{a+b}=F_{a+1}F_b+F_aF_{b-1}$, so
$$\gcd(F_{a+b},F_b)=\gcd(F_aF_{b-1},F_b)=\gcd(F_a,F_b),$$
where the last equality holds because $F_{b-1}$ and $F_b$ are coprime (Step 2).

**Step 4: Euclid on the indices.** By Step 3, $\gcd(F_m,F_n)=\gcd(F_{m-n},F_n)$ for $m>n$. This is the subtraction form of the Euclidean algorithm, applied to the indices, which ends at $\gcd(F_g,F_0)=F_g$ with $g=\gcd(m,n)$. Hence $\gcd(F_m,F_n)=F_{\gcd(m,n)}$. $\blacksquare$

**Consequence.** Let $n>4$ be composite, $n=ab$ with $2\le a\le b$. Since $n\ne4$, $b\ge3$, and $b<n$. By the formula, $\gcd(F_b,F_n)=F_{\gcd(b,n)}=F_b$, i.e. $F_b\mid F_n$. Here $F_b\ge F_3=2$ and $F_b<F_n$, so $F_n$ has a proper divisor $F_b>1$ and is composite. Hence if $F_n$ is prime and $n>4$, then $n$ is prime.

**Remark.** The converse fails: $F_{19}=4181=37\cdot113$.

**Lesson.** A divisibility property that is "Euclid-like" (gcd of values equals value at the gcd) is proved by showing $\gcd(f(a+b),f(b))=\gcd(f(a),f(b))$.
@@@ alg2-cassini-and-sums-of-squares-of-fibonacci-numbers.md
---
title: Cassini's identity and sums of Fibonacci squares
category: algebra/equations
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: Fibonacci numbers, matrices, determinant, induction
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $F_0=0$, $F_1=1$, $F_{n+1}=F_n+F_{n-1}$. Prove that for $n\ge1$:
1. $F_{n+1}F_{n-1}-F_n^2=(-1)^n$ (Cassini),
2. $F_1^2+F_2^2+\cdots+F_n^2=F_nF_{n+1}$,
3. $F_{n}^2+F_{n+1}^2=F_{2n+1}$.

Compute $F_1^2+\dots+F_{10}^2$.

## Hint

For part 1, consider the matrix $M=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ and its powers. For part 2, use induction.

## Solution

**1.** By induction, $M^n=\begin{pmatrix}F_{n+1}&F_n\\F_n&F_{n-1}\end{pmatrix}$. Taking determinants, $\det M^n=(\det M)^n=(-1)^n$, i.e. $F_{n+1}F_{n-1}-F_n^2=(-1)^n$.

**2.** For $n=1$: $F_1^2=1=F_1F_2$. If the formula holds for $n$, then
$$F_nF_{n+1}+F_{n+1}^2=F_{n+1}(F_n+F_{n+1})=F_{n+1}F_{n+2}.$$

**3.** $M^{2n+1}=M^nM^{n+1}$, and the $(1,2)$ entry of $M^k$ is $F_k$. The $(1,2)$ entry of the product is $F_{n+1}\cdot F_{n+1}+F_n\cdot F_n$. Hence $F_{2n+1}=F_n^2+F_{n+1}^2$.

**Computation.** $F_{10}=55$, $F_{11}=89$, so $\sum_{k=1}^{10}F_k^2=55\cdot89=4895$.

**Lesson.** Powers of the companion matrix $M$ package the Fibonacci recurrence: determinants and products of powers give identities for free.
@@@ alg2-the-continued-fraction-of-the-square-root-of-two.md
---
title: Approximating √2 by 1 + 1/(1 + x)
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: continued fractions, sequences, Pell equation, approximation
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Define $x_0=1$ and $x_{n+1}=1+\dfrac1{1+x_n}$. Write $x_n=\dfrac{p_n}{q_n}$ with $p_0=q_0=1$ and $p_{n+1}=p_n+2q_n$, $q_{n+1}=p_n+q_n$.

1. Prove that $x_n\to\sqrt2$ and estimate the error.
2. Prove that $p_n^2-2q_n^2=(-1)^{n+1}$.
3. Deduce that $\left|\sqrt2-\dfrac{p_n}{q_n}\right|<\dfrac1{2q_n^2}$.

## Hint

For part 1, compute $x_{n+1}-\sqrt2$ in terms of $x_n-\sqrt2$. For part 2, compute $p_{n+1}^2-2q_{n+1}^2$ from the recurrences.

## Solution

**Consistency.** $1+\frac1{1+p/q}=1+\frac q{p+q}=\frac{p+2q}{p+q}$, so $x_n=\frac{p_n}{q_n}$ indeed.

**1.** Since $x_{n+1}=\frac{2+x_n}{1+x_n}$,
$$x_{n+1}-\sqrt2=\frac{2+x_n-\sqrt2-\sqrt2x_n}{1+x_n}=\frac{(1-\sqrt2)(x_n-\sqrt2)}{1+x_n}.$$
All $x_n\ge1$, so
$$|x_{n+1}-\sqrt2|\le\frac{\sqrt2-1}{2}|x_n-\sqrt2|<\frac{|x_n-\sqrt2|}{4}.$$
Hence $|x_n-\sqrt2|\le4^{-n}|x_0-\sqrt2|\to0$: geometric convergence.

**2.**
$$p_{n+1}^2-2q_{n+1}^2=(p_n+2q_n)^2-2(p_n+q_n)^2=-p_n^2+2q_n^2=-(p_n^2-2q_n^2).$$
The sign alternates, and $p_0^2-2q_0^2=-1$, so $p_n^2-2q_n^2=(-1)^{n+1}$.

**3.** $\left|\sqrt2-\frac{p_n}{q_n}\right|=\dfrac{|2q_n^2-p_n^2|}{q_n^2\left(\sqrt2+\frac{p_n}{q_n}\right)}=\dfrac1{q_n^2\left(\sqrt2+x_n\right)}<\dfrac1{2q_n^2}$, since $\sqrt2+x_n>\sqrt2+1>2$.

**First terms.** $1,\ \frac32,\ \frac75,\ \frac{17}{12},\ \frac{41}{29},\dots$ and $3^2-2\cdot2^2=1$, $7^2-2\cdot5^2=-1$, $17^2-2\cdot12^2=1$.

**Lesson.** An iteration $x\mapsto\frac{ax+b}{cx+d}$ with a fixed point $\alpha$ contracts: compute $x_{n+1}-\alpha$ as a multiple of $x_n-\alpha$.
@@@ alg2-an-integer-polynomial-with-root-sqrt2-plus-sqrt3-plus-sqrt5.md
---
title: A monic polynomial with root √2 + √3 + √5
category: algebra/polynomials
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: algebraic numbers, minimal polynomial, rational root theorem, irrationality
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

1. Find a monic polynomial with integer coefficients having $\sqrt2+\sqrt3$ as a root.
2. Do the same for $s=\sqrt2+\sqrt3+\sqrt5$, with a polynomial of degree $8$.
3. Prove that $s$ is irrational.

## Hint

Isolate one square root and square, repeatedly. For part 3 use the rational root theorem: a rational root of a monic integer polynomial is an integer.

## Solution

**1.** Let $t=\sqrt2+\sqrt3$. Then $t^2=5+2\sqrt6$, so $(t^2-5)^2=24$, i.e.
$$t^4-10t^2+1=0.$$

**2.** Let $x=\sqrt2+\sqrt3+\sqrt5=t+\sqrt5$, so $t=x-\sqrt5$. Substituting:
$$(x-\sqrt5)^4-10(x-\sqrt5)^2+1=0.$$
Expand: $(x-\sqrt5)^4=x^4-4\sqrt5x^3+30x^2-20\sqrt5x+25$ and $-10(x-\sqrt5)^2=-10x^2+20\sqrt5x-50$. The terms in $x$ cancel and
$$x^4+20x^2-24=4\sqrt5\,x^3.$$
Squaring: $(x^4+20x^2-24)^2=80x^6$, that is
$$x^8-40x^6+352x^4-960x^2+576=0.$$
So $s$ is a root of this monic integer polynomial of degree $8$.

**3.** By the rational root theorem, a rational root of this monic polynomial is an integer. But $5.37<s<5.40$: $s$ lies strictly between $5$ and $6$ and is not an integer. Hence $s$ is irrational. $\blacksquare$

**Remark.** The degree $8$ is the best possible: the $8$ numbers $\pm\sqrt2\pm\sqrt3\pm\sqrt5$ are the roots of this polynomial.

**Lesson.** To find a polynomial killing a sum of square roots, eliminate the radicals one at a time.
@@@ alg2-complex-numbers-of-modulus-one-and-symmetric-expressions.md
---
title: Three complex numbers of modulus one
category: algebra/complex-numbers
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: complex numbers, unit circle, conjugation, equilateral triangle
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a,b,c$ be complex numbers with $|a|=|b|=|c|=1$.

1. Prove that $|ab+bc+ca|=|a+b+c|$.
2. Prove that if $a+b+c=0$, then $a,b,c$ are the vertices of an equilateral triangle.
3. Prove that $\dfrac{(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)}{abc}$ is a real number.

## Hint

On the unit circle, $\bar a=\frac1a$. Express $\overline{a+b+c}$ in terms of $ab+bc+ca$.

## Solution

**1.** Since $\bar a=1/a$, etc.,
$$\overline{a+b+c}=\frac1a+\frac1b+\frac1c=\frac{ab+bc+ca}{abc}.$$
Taking moduli and using $|abc|=1$: $|a+b+c|=|ab+bc+ca|$.

**2.** If $a+b+c=0$, part 1 gives $ab+bc+ca=0$. Then $a,b,c$ are the roots of $t^3-abc=0$. The equation $t^3=abc$ has three distinct roots (since $abc\ne0$): the three cube roots of $abc$, which are $r,r\omega,r\omega^2$ — vertices of an equilateral triangle centred at $0$.

**3.** Let $Z=\dfrac{(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)}{abc}$. For $a,b\ne0$: $\overline{a+b}=\frac1a+\frac1b=\frac{a+b}{ab}$. Therefore
$$\overline{(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)}=\frac{(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)}{(abc)^2},\qquad\overline{abc}=\frac1{abc}.$$
Hence $\bar Z=\dfrac{(a+b)(b+c)(c+a)}{(abc)^2}\cdot abc=Z$, so $Z$ is real. $\blacksquare$

**Lesson.** On the unit circle replace $\bar z$ by $1/z$: symmetric expressions become rational functions you can compare with their conjugates.
@@@ alg2-tan-20-tan-40-tan-80-and-a-sum-of-squares.md
---
title: tan 20° tan 40° tan 80° and tan²20° + tan²40° + tan²80°
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: trigonometry, tangent, triple angle, Vieta
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

1. Prove that $\tan\theta\,\tan(60^\circ-\theta)\,\tan(60^\circ+\theta)=\tan3\theta$.
2. Deduce $\tan20^\circ\tan40^\circ\tan80^\circ=\sqrt3$.
3. Prove that $\tan^220^\circ+\tan^240^\circ+\tan^280^\circ=33$.

## Hint

For part 1, with $t=\tan\theta$: $\tan(60^\circ\pm\theta)=\frac{\sqrt3\pm t}{1\mp\sqrt3t}$. For part 3, find a cubic equation for which $\tan20^\circ$, $\tan80^\circ$ and $-\tan40^\circ$ are the three roots.

## Solution

**1.** With $t=\tan\theta$,
$$\tan(60^\circ+\theta)\tan(60^\circ-\theta)=\frac{(\sqrt3+t)(\sqrt3-t)}{(1-\sqrt3t)(1+\sqrt3t)}=\frac{3-t^2}{1-3t^2},$$
so the product is $\dfrac{t(3-t^2)}{1-3t^2}=\tan3\theta$ (the triple-angle formula).

**2.** For $\theta=20^\circ$: $60^\circ-20^\circ=40^\circ$ and $60^\circ+20^\circ=80^\circ$, so the product is $\tan60^\circ=\sqrt3$.

**3.** The equation $\tan3\theta=\sqrt3$ means $\frac{3t-t^3}{1-3t^2}=\sqrt3$, i.e.
$$t^3-3\sqrt3\,t^2-3t+\sqrt3=0.$$
Its solutions $t=\tan\theta$ include $\theta=20^\circ,\ 80^\circ,\ 140^\circ$ (since $3\theta=60^\circ,240^\circ,420^\circ$, all with tangent $\sqrt3$). These give the three roots $\tan20^\circ$, $\tan80^\circ$, $\tan140^\circ=-\tan40^\circ$. By Vieta, with $e_1=3\sqrt3$ and $e_2=-3$:
$$\tan^220^\circ+\tan^280^\circ+\tan^2(-40^\circ)=e_1^2-2e_2=27+6=33.$$
Since $\tan^2(-40^\circ)=\tan^240^\circ$, this is the required sum. $\blacksquare$

**Lesson.** When several trigonometric values share an angle equation ($\tan3\theta=\text{const}$), they are the roots of one polynomial: use Vieta for their symmetric functions.
@@@ alg2-the-product-of-cosines-of-multiples-of-pi-over-2n-plus-1.md
---
title: The product cos(π/N)·cos(2π/N)···cos(nπ/N) with N = 2n + 1
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: trigonometry, product, double angle, permutation of angles
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge1$ and $N=2n+1$. Prove that
$$\prod_{k=1}^n\cos\frac{k\pi}N=\frac1{2^n}.$$

## Hint

Multiply by $\prod\sin\frac{k\pi}N$ and use $\sin2x=2\sin x\cos x$. Then show that $\{\sin\frac{2k\pi}N\}_{k\le n}$ is a rearrangement of $\{\sin\frac{j\pi}N\}_{j\le n}$ using $\sin x=\sin(\pi-x)$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Doubling the angle turns the cosine product into a sine product that can be compared with the original sine product.

**Step 1.** Let $S=\prod_{k=1}^n\sin\frac{k\pi}N>0$ and $C=\prod_{k=1}^n\cos\frac{k\pi}N$. Then
$$\prod_{k=1}^n\sin\frac{2k\pi}N=2^nSC.$$

**Step 2: the left side equals $S$.** For each $k\in\{1,\dots,n\}$, either $2k\le n$, then $\sin\frac{2k\pi}N$ is already a factor of $S$; or $2k>n$, then
$$\sin\frac{2k\pi}N=\sin\left(\pi-\frac{2k\pi}N\right)=\sin\frac{(N-2k)\pi}N,\qquad N-2k=2(n-k)+1\in\{1,3,\dots\}.$$
Here $j=N-2k$ satisfies $1\le j\le n$. The indices $2k$ (for $2k\le n$) are the even numbers in $\{1,\dots,n\}$; the indices $N-2k$ (for $2k>n$) are the odd numbers in $\{1,\dots,n\}$. Each integer in $\{1,\dots,n\}$ appears exactly once, so the left side equals $S$.

**Step 3.** Therefore $2^nSC=S$, and since $S\ne0$, $C=2^{-n}$. $\blacksquare$

**Check.** $n=3$ ($N=7$): $\cos\frac\pi7\cos\frac{2\pi}7\cos\frac{3\pi}7=\frac18$ ✓.

**Lesson.** To evaluate a product of trigonometric values at equally spaced angles, double the angles and show that the new product is a permutation of the old one.
@@@ alg2-telescoping-sums-with-factorials-and-products.md
---
title: Three telescoping sums
category: algebra/equations
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: telescoping, factorials, partial fractions
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Compute, for $n\ge1$:
1. $\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^nk\cdot k!$,
2. $\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^n\frac k{(k+1)!}$,
3. $\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^n\frac1{k(k+1)(k+2)}$.

## Hint

Write each term as a difference $u_{k+1}-u_k$ for a simple $u_k$: for (1), $k\cdot k!=(k+1)!-k!$; for (3), use partial fractions.

## Solution

**1.** $k\cdot k!=(k+1-1)k!=(k+1)!-k!$. The sum telescopes:
$$\sum_{k=1}^nk\cdot k!=(n+1)!-1.$$

**2.** $\dfrac k{(k+1)!}=\dfrac{(k+1)-1}{(k+1)!}=\dfrac1{k!}-\dfrac1{(k+1)!}$, so
$$\sum_{k=1}^n\frac k{(k+1)!}=1-\frac1{(n+1)!}.$$

**3.** $\dfrac1{k(k+1)(k+2)}=\dfrac12\left(\dfrac1{k(k+1)}-\dfrac1{(k+1)(k+2)}\right)$, so
$$\sum_{k=1}^n\frac1{k(k+1)(k+2)}=\frac12\left(\frac12-\frac1{(n+1)(n+2)}\right)=\frac{n(n+3)}{4(n+1)(n+2)}.$$

**Check.** $n=1$: (1) $1=2!-1$; (2) $\frac12=1-\frac12$; (3) $\frac16=\frac{4}{4\cdot2\cdot3}$ ✓.

**Lesson.** Make terms telescope by adding and subtracting a suitable quantity.
@@@ alg2-growth-of-the-sequence-a-n-plus-one-equals-a-n-plus-one-over-a-n.md
---
title: How fast does a_{n+1} = a_n + 1/a_n grow?
category: algebra/equations
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: sequences, growth, squaring trick, estimates
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $a_1=1$ and $a_{n+1}=a_n+\dfrac1{a_n}$.

1. Prove that $2n-1\le a_n^2\le2n+\ln n$ for all $n\ge1$.
2. Deduce that $\dfrac{a_n}{\sqrt{2n}}\to1$.
3. Show that $14<a_{100}<15$.

## Hint

Square the recurrence: $a_{n+1}^2=a_n^2+2+\frac1{a_n^2}$.

## Solution

**Squaring.** $a_{n+1}^2=a_n^2+2+\dfrac1{a_n^2}$. All $a_n\ge1>0$.

**Lower bound.** Since $\frac1{a_n^2}>0$, $a_{n+1}^2>a_n^2+2$, so $a_n^2\ge a_1^2+2(n-1)=2n-1$.

**Upper bound.** Summing the squared recurrence from $1$ to $n-1$,
$$a_n^2=1+2(n-1)+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac1{a_k^2}\le2n-1+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac1{2k-1},$$
using $a_k^2\ge2k-1$. Since $\frac1{2k-1}\le\frac1k$ for $k\ge1$, the last sum is at most $H_{n-1}\le1+\ln(n-1)$ for $n\ge2$. Thus
$$a_n^2\le2n-1+1+\ln n=2n+\ln n.$$

**2.** Dividing by $2n$: $1-\frac1{2n}\le\frac{a_n^2}{2n}\le1+\frac{\ln n}{2n}$, and both bounds tend to $1$. Hence $\frac{a_n}{\sqrt{2n}}\to1$.

**3.** $a_{100}^2\ge199>196=14^2$ and $a_{100}^2\le200+\ln100<205<225=15^2$. So $14<a_{100}<15$.

**Lesson.** For a recurrence $a_{n+1}=a_n+\varepsilon_n$ with small increments, look at $a_n^2$ so that the main term becomes linear.
@@@ alg2-the-derivative-inequality-for-polynomials-with-real-roots.md
---
title: P′² ≥ P·P″ for polynomials with real roots
category: algebra/inequalities
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: polynomials, real roots, logarithmic derivative, inequalities
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $P$ be a real polynomial of degree $n\ge1$ all of whose roots are real. Prove that for every real $x$,
$$P'(x)^2\ge P(x)\,P''(x),$$
with strict inequality when $x$ is not a root of $P$.

## Hint

If $P(x)=c\prod(x-r_i)$ and $x$ is not a root, compute $\dfrac{P'}P$ and then its derivative.

## Solution

**Idea.** The logarithmic derivative $\frac{P'}P=\sum\frac1{x-r_i}$ is a sum of terms whose derivatives are all negative.

**Away from the roots.** Let $P(x)=c\prod_{i=1}^n(x-r_i)$ with real $r_i$ and $c\ne0$. For $x\notin\{r_i\}$,
$$\frac{P'(x)}{P(x)}=\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{x-r_i}.$$
Differentiating,
$$\frac{P''(x)P(x)-P'(x)^2}{P(x)^2}=-\sum_{i=1}^n\frac1{(x-r_i)^2}<0.$$
Multiplying by $P(x)^2>0$: $P'(x)^2-P(x)P''(x)=P(x)^2\sum_i\frac1{(x-r_i)^2}>0$.

**At a root.** If $P(x)=0$ then $P(x)P''(x)=0\le P'(x)^2$. $\blacksquare$

**Example.** For $P(x)=x^2-1$: $P'^2-PP''=2x^2+2>0$ ✓. For $P=x^2+1$ (no real roots): $4x^2-2(x^2+1)=2x^2-2<0$ at $x=0$, so the hypothesis cannot be dropped.

**Lesson.** The logarithmic derivative turns a product into a sum, and its derivative $-\sum\frac1{(x-r_i)^2}$ has a definite sign when the roots are real.
